background-color:transparent; position:relative; top:-4px; z-index:100;"><div style="position:absolute; top:-4px; left:245.23076923077px; width:0px; border-top: 8px solid #360; border-left: 4px solid transparent; href="./Category:Articles_with_'species'_microformats"/></span><p id="mwDg"><b id="mwDw">Zebras</b> (<span class="rt-commentedText nowrap" about="#mwt16" typeof="mw:Transclusion" id="mwEA" data-mw='{"parts":[{"template":{"target":{"wt":"IPAc-en","href":"./Template:IPAc-en"},"params":{"1":{"wt":"US"},"2":{"wt":"ˈ"},"3":{"wt":"z"},"4":{"wt":"iː"},"5":{"wt":"b"},"6":{"wt":"r"},"7":{"wt":"ə"},"8":{"wt":"z"}},"i":0}}]}'><style data-mw-deduplicate="TemplateStyles:r1177148991" typeof="mw:Extension/templatestyles" about="#mwt17" data-mw='{"name":"templatestyles","attrs":{"src":"Module:IPA/styles.css"},"body":{"extsrc":""}}'>.mw-parser-output .IPA-label-small{font-size:85%}.mw-parser-output .references .IPA-label-small,.mw-parser-output .infobox .IPA-label-small,.mw-parser-output .navbox .IPA-label-small{font-size:100%}</style><span class="IPA-label IPA-label-small"><a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="//en.wikipedia.org/wiki/American_English" title="American English">US</a>: </span><span class="IPA nopopups noexcerpt" lang="en-fonipa"><a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="//en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Help:IPA/English" title="Help:IPA/English">/<span style="border-bottom:1px dotted"><span title="/ˈ/: primary stress follows">ˈ</span><span title="'z' in 'zoom'">z</span><span title="/iː/: 'ee' in 'fleece'">iː</span><span title="'b' in 'buy'">b</span><span title="'r' in 'rye'">r</span><span title="/ə/: 'a' in 'about'">ə</span><span title="'z' in 'zoom'">z</span></span>/</a></span></span>, <span class="rt-commentedText nowrap" about="#mwt18" typeof="mw:Transclusion" id="mwEQ" data-mw='{"parts":[{"template":{"target":{"wt":"IPAc-en","href":"./Template:IPAc-en"},"params":{"1":{"wt":"UK"},"2":{"wt":"ˈ"},"3":{"wt":"z"},"4":{"wt":"ɛ"},"5":{"wt":"b"},"6":{"wt":"r"},"7":{"wt":"ə"},"8":{"wt":"z"},"9":{"wt":",_"},"10":{"wt":"ˈ"},"11":{"wt":"z"},"12":{"wt":"iː"},"13":{"wt":"-"}},"i":0}}]}'><link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1177148991" about="#mwt19" typeof="mw:Extension/templatestyles" data-mw='{"name":"templatestyles","attrs":{"src":"Module:IPA/styles.css"},"body":{"extsrc":""}}'/><span class="IPA-label IPA-label-small"><a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="//en.wikipedia.org/wiki/British_English" title="British English">UK</a>: </span><span class="IPA nopopups noexcerpt" lang="en-fonipa"><a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="//en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Help:IPA/English" title="Help:IPA/English">/<span style="border-bottom:1px dotted"><span title="/ˈ/: primary stress follows">ˈ</span><span title="'z' in 'zoom'">z</span><span title="/ɛ/: 'e' in 'dress'">ɛ</span><span title="'b' in 'buy'">b</span><span title="'r' in 'rye'">r</span><span title="/ə/: 'a' in 'about'">ə</span><span title="'z' in 'zoom'">z</span></span>,<span class="wrap"> </span><span style="border-bottom:1px dotted"><span title="/ˈ/: primary stress follows">ˈ</span><span title="'z' in 'zoom'">z</span><span title="/iː/: 'ee' in 'fleece'">iː</span></span>-/</a></span></span>)<sup about="#mwt22" class="mw-ref reference" id="cite_ref-2" rel="dc:references" typeof="mw:Extension/ref" data-mw='{"name":"ref","attrs":{},"body":{"id":"mw-reference-text-cite_note-2"}}'><a href="#cite_note-2" id="mwEg"><span class="mw-reflink-text" id="mwEw"><span class="cite-bracket" id="mwFA">[</span>2<span class="cite-bracket" id="mwFQ">]</span></span></a></sup> (subgenus <i id="mwFg"><b id="mwFw">Hippotigris</b></i>) are African <a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="//en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Equines" title="Equines" class="mw-redirect" id="mwGA">equines</a> with distinctive black-and-white striped <a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="//en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Animal_coat" title="Animal coat" id="mwGQ">coats</a>. There are three <a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="//en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Extant_taxon" title="Extant taxon" class="mw-redirect" id="mwGg">living</a> species: <a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="//en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Grévy's_zebra" title="Grévy's zebra" id="mwGw">Grévy's zebra</a> (<i id="mwHA">Equus grevyi</i>), the <a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="//en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Plains_zebra" title="Plains zebra" id="mwHQ">plains zebra</a> (<i id="mwHg">E. quagga</i>), and the <a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="//en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mountain_zebra" title="Mountain zebra" id="mwHw">mountain zebra</a> (<i id="mwIA">E. zebra</i>). Zebras share the genus <i id="mwIQ"><a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="//en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Equus_(genus)" title="Equus (genus)" id="mwIg">Equus</a></i> with <a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="//en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wild_horse" title="Wild horse" id="mwIw">horses</a> and <a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="//en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Asinus" title="Asinus" id="mwJA">asses</a>, the three groups being the only living members of the family <a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="//en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Equidae" title="Equidae" id="mwJQ">Equidae</a>. Zebra stripes come in different patterns, unique to each individual. Several theories have been proposed for the function of these patterns, with most evidence supporting them as a deterrent for biting flies. Zebras inhabit <a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="//en.wikipedia.org/wiki/East_Africa" title="East Africa" id="mwJg">eastern</a> and <a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="//en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Southern_Africa" title="Southern Africa" id="mwJw">southern Africa</a> and can be found in a variety of habitats such as <a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="//en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Savannahs" title="Savannahs" class="mw-redirect" id="mwKA">savannahs</a>, <a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="//en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Grasslands" title="Grasslands" class="mw-redirect" id="mwKQ">grasslands</a>, woodlands, <a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="//en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Shrubland" title="Shrubland" id="mwKg">shrublands</a>, and mountainous areas.</p><p id="mwKw">Zebras are primarily <a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="//en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Grazing_(behaviour)" title="Grazing (behaviour)" id="mwLA">grazers</a> and can subsist on lower-quality <a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="//en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vegetation" title="Vegetation" id="mwLQ">vegetation</a>. They are preyed on mainly by <a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="//en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lion" title="Lion" id="mwLg">lions</a>, and typically flee when threatened but also bite and kick. Zebra species differ in <a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="//en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sociality" title="Sociality" id="mwLw">social behaviour</a>, with plains and mountain zebra living in stable <a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="//en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Harem_(zoology)" title="Harem (zoology)" id="mwMA">harems</a> consisting of an adult male or <a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="//en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Stallion" title="Stallion" id="mwMQ">stallion</a>, several adult females or <a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="//en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mares" title="Mares" class="mw-redirect" id="mwMg">mares</a>, and their young or <a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="//en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Foals" title="Foals" class="mw-redirect" id="mwMw">foals</a>; while Grévy's zebra live alone or in loosely associated herds. In harem-holding species, adult females mate only with their harem stallion, while male Grévy's zebras establish <a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="//en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Territory_(animal)" title="Territory (animal)" id="mwNA">territories</a> which attract females and the species is <a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="//en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Polygynandry" title="Polygynandry" id="mwNQ">promiscuous</a>. Zebras communicate with various vocalisations, body postures and facial expressions. <a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="//en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Social_grooming" title="Social grooming" id="mwNg">Social grooming</a> strengthens social bonds in plains and mountain zebras.</p><p id="mwNw">Zebras' distinctive stripes make them among the most recognisable mammals. They have been featured in art and stories in Africa and beyond. Historically, they have been highly sought by exotic animal collectors, but unlike <a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="//en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Horses" title="Horses" class="mw-redirect" id="mwOA">horses</a> and <a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="//en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Donkeys" title="Donkeys" class="mw-redirect" id="mwOQ">donkeys</a>, zebras have never been completely <a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="//en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Domesticated" title="Domesticated" class="mw-redirect" id="mwOg">domesticated</a>. The <a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="//en.wikipedia.org/wiki/International_Union_for_Conservation_of_Nature" title="International Union for Conservation of Nature" id="mwOw">International Union for Conservation of Nature</a> (IUCN) lists Grévy's zebra as <a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="//en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Endangered_species" title="Endangered species" id="mwPA">endangered</a>, the mountain zebra as <a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="//en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vulnerable_species" title="Vulnerable species" id="mwPQ">vulnerable</a> and the plains zebra as <a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="//en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Near-threatened_species" title="Near-threatened species" id="mwPg">near-threatened</a>. The <a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="//en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Quagga" title="Quagga" id="mwPw">quagga</a> (<i id="mwQA">E. quagga quagga</i>), a type of plains zebra, was driven to extinction in the 19th century. Nevertheless, zebras can be found in numerous protected areas.</p><meta property="mw:PageProp/toc" id="mwQQ" data-mw='{"autoGenerated":true}'/></section><section data-mw-section-id="1" id="mwQg" aria-labelledby="Etymology"><div class="mw-heading mw-heading2"><h2 id="Etymology">Etymology</h2></div><p id="mwQw">The English name "zebra" derives from <a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="//en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Italian_language" title="Italian language" id="mwRA">Italian</a>, <a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="//en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Spanish_language" title="Spanish language" id="mwRQ">Spanish</a> or <a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="//en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Portuguese_language" title="Portuguese language" id="mwRg">Portuguese</a>.<sup about="#mwt28" class="mw-ref reference" id="cite_ref-etymology_3-0" rel="dc:references" typeof="mw:Extension/ref" data-mw='{"name":"ref","attrs":{"name":"etymology"},"body":{"id":"mw-reference-text-cite_note-etymology-3"}}'><a href="#cite_note-etymology-3" id="mwRw"><span class="mw-reflink-text" id="mwSA"><span class="cite-bracket" id="mwSQ">[</span>3<span class="cite-bracket" id="mwSg">]</span></span></a></sup><sup about="#mwt31" class="mw-ref reference" id="cite_ref-4" rel="dc:references" typeof="mw:Extension/ref" data-mw='{"name":"ref","attrs":{},"body":{"id":"mw-reference-text-cite_note-4"}}'><a href="#cite_note-4" id="mwSw"><span class="mw-reflink-text" id="mwTA"><span class="cite-bracket" id="mwTQ">[</span>4<span class="cite-bracket" id="mwTg">]</span></span></a></sup> Its origins may lie in the <a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="//en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Latin" title="Latin" id="mwTw">Latin</a> <i id="mwUA">equiferus</i>, meaning "wild horse". <i id="mwUQ">Equiferus</i> appears to have entered into Portuguese as <i id="mwUg">ezebro</i> or <i id="mwUw">zebro</i>, which was originally used for a <a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="//en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Zebro" title="Zebro" id="mwVA">mysterious equine</a> reported in the wilds of the <a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="//en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Iberian_Peninsula" title="Iberian Peninsula" id="mwVQ">Iberian Peninsula</a> during the Middle Ages. In 1591, Italian explorer <a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="//en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Filippo_Pigafetta" title="Filippo Pigafetta" id="mwVg">Filippo Pigafetta</a> recorded "zebra" being used to refer to the African animals by Portuguese visitors to the continent.<sup about="#mwt34" class="mw-ref reference" id="cite_ref-zebro_5-0" rel="dc:references" typeof="mw:Extension/ref" data-mw='{"name":"ref","attrs":{"name":"zebro"},"body":{"id":"mw-reference-text-cite_note-zebro-5"}}'><a href="#cite_note-zebro-5" id="mwVw"><span class="mw-reflink-text" id="mwWA"><span class="cite-bracket" id="mwWQ">[</span>5<span class="cite-bracket" id="mwWg">]</span></span></a></sup> In ancient times, the zebra was called <i id="mwWw">hippotigris</i> ("horse tiger") by the <a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="//en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ancient_Greece" title="Ancient Greece" id="mwXA">Greeks</a> and <a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="//en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ancient_Rome" title="Ancient Rome" id="mwXQ">Romans</a>.<sup about="#mwt35" class="mw-ref reference" id="cite_ref-zebro_5-1" rel="dc:references" typeof="mw:Extension/ref" data-mw='{"name":"ref","attrs":{"name":"zebro"}}'><a href="#cite_note-zebro-5" id="mwXg"><span class="mw-reflink-text" id="mwXw"><span class="cite-bracket" id="mwYA">[</span>5<span class="cite-bracket" id="mwYQ">]</span></span></a></sup><sup about="#mwt23" class="mw-ref reference" id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEPlumbShaw201854_6-0" rel="dc:references" typeof="mw:Transclusion mw:Extension/ref" data-mw='{"name":"ref","attrs":{"group":"","name":"FOOTNOTEPlumbShaw201854"},"body":{"id":"mw-reference-text-cite_note-FOOTNOTEPlumbShaw201854-6"},"parts":[{"template":{"target":{"wt":"sfn","href":"./Template:Sfn"},"params":{"1":{"wt":"Plumb"},"2":{"wt":"Shaw"},"3":{"wt":"2018"},"p":{"wt":"54"}},"i":0}}]}'><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEPlumbShaw201854-6" id="mwYg"><span class="mw-reflink-text" id="mwYw"><span class="cite-bracket" id="mwZA">[</span>6<span class="cite-bracket" id="mwZQ">]</span></span></a></sup></p><p id="mwZg">The word <i id="mwZw">zebra</i> was traditionally pronounced with a long initial vowel, but over the course of the 20th century the pronunciation with the short initial vowel became the norm in <a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="//en.wikipedia.org/wiki/British_English" title="British English" id="mwaA">British English</a>.<sup about="#mwt38" class="mw-ref reference" id="cite_ref-7" rel="dc:references" typeof="mw:Extension/ref" data-mw='{"name":"ref","attrs":{},"body":{"id":"mw-reference-text-cite_note-7"}}'><a href="#cite_note-7" id="mwaQ"><span class="mw-reflink-text" id="mwag"><span class="cite-bracket" id="mwaw">[</span>7<span class="cite-bracket" id="mwbA">]</span></span></a></sup> The pronunciation with a long initial vowel remains standard in <a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="//en.wikipedia.org/wiki/American_English" title="American English" id="mwbQ">American English</a>.<sup about="#mwt41" class="mw-ref reference" id="cite_ref-8" rel="dc:references" typeof="mw:Extension/ref" data-mw='{"name":"ref","attrs":{},"body":{"id":"mw-reference-text-cite_note-8"}}'><a href="#cite_note-8" id="mwbg"><span class="mw-reflink-text" id="mwbw"><span class="cite-bracket" id="mwcA">[</span>8<span class="cite-bracket" id="mwcQ">]</span></span></a></sup></p></section><section data-mw-section-id="2" id="mwcg" aria-labelledby="Taxonomy"><div class="mw-heading mw-heading2"><h2 id="Taxonomy">Taxonomy</h2></div><span class="mw-empty-elt" about="#mwt42" typeof="mw:Transclusion" id="mwcw" data-mw='{"parts":[{"template":{"target":{"wt":"Further","href":"./Template:Further"},"params":{"1":{"wt":"Evolution of the horse"}},"i":0}}]}'><link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1353705441" about="#mwt43" typeof="mw:Extension/templatestyles" data-mw='{"name":"templatestyles","attrs":{"src":"Module:Hatnote/styles.css"},"body":{"extsrc":""}}'/></span><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable" about="#mwt42" id="mwdA">Further information: <a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="//en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Evolution_of_the_horse" title="Evolution of the horse">Evolution of the horse</a></div><p id="mwdQ">Zebras are classified in the genus <i id="mwdg"><a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="//en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Equus_(genus)" title="Equus (genus)" id="mwdw">Equus</a></i> (known as equines) along with <a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="//en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wild_horse" title="Wild horse" id="mweA">horses</a> and <a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="//en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Asinus" title="Asinus" id="mweQ">asses</a>. These three groups are the only living members of the family <a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="//en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Equidae" title="Equidae" id="mweg">Equidae</a>.<sup about="#mwt44" class="mw-ref reference" id="cite_ref-MacDonald_9-0" rel="dc:references" typeof="mw:Extension/ref" data-mw='{"name":"ref","attrs":{"name":"MacDonald"}}'><a href="#cite_note-MacDonald-9" id="mwew"><span class="mw-reflink-text" id="mwfA"><span class="cite-bracket" id="mwfQ">[</span>9<span class="cite-bracket" id="mwfg">]</span></span></a></sup> The <a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="//en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Plains_zebra" title="Plains zebra" id="mwfw">plains zebra</a> and <a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="//en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mountain_zebra" title="Mountain zebra" id="mwgA">mountain zebra</a> were traditionally placed in the <a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="//en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Subgenus" title="Subgenus" id="mwgQ">subgenus</a> <i id="mwgg">Hippotigris</i> (C. H. Smith, 1841) in contrast to the <a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="//en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Grévy's_zebra" title="Grévy's zebra" id="mwgw">Grévy's zebra</a> which was considered the sole species of subgenus <i id="mwhA">Dolichohippus</i> (Heller, 1912).<sup about="#mwt47" class="mw-ref reference" id="cite_ref-Prothero_2003_10-0" rel="dc:references" typeof="mw:Extension/ref" data-mw='{"name":"ref","attrs":{"name":"Prothero 2003"},"body":{"id":"mw-reference-text-cite_note-Prothero_2003-10"}}'><a href="#cite_note-Prothero_2003-10" id="mwhQ"><span class="mw-reflink-text" id="mwhg"><span class="cite-bracket" id="mwhw">[</span>10<span class="cite-bracket" id="mwiA">]</span></span></a></sup><sup about="#mwt50" class="mw-ref reference" id="cite_ref-11" rel="dc:references" typeof="mw:Extension/ref" data-mw='{"name":"ref","attrs":{},"body":{"id":"mw-reference-text-cite_note-11"}}'><a href="#cite_note-11" id="mwiQ"><span class="mw-reflink-text" id="mwig"><span class="cite-bracket" id="mwiw">[</span>11<span class="cite-bracket" id="mwjA">]</span></span></a></sup><sup about="#mwt53" class="mw-ref reference" id="cite_ref-12" rel="dc:references" typeof="mw:Extension/ref" data-mw='{"name":"ref","attrs":{},"body":{"id":"mw-reference-text-cite_note-12"}}'><a href="#cite_note-12" id="mwjQ"><span class="mw-reflink-text" id="mwjg"><span class="cite-bracket" id="mwjw">[</span>12<span class="cite-bracket" id="mwkA">]</span></span></a></sup> Groves and Bell (2004) placed all three species in the subgenus <i id="mwkQ">Hippotigris</i>.<sup about="#mwt56" class="mw-ref reference" id="cite_ref-GrovesBell2004_13-0" rel="dc:references" typeof="mw:Extension/ref" data-mw='{"name":"ref","attrs":{"name":"GrovesBell2004"},"body":{"id":"mw-reference-text-cite_note-GrovesBell2004-13"}}'><a href="#cite_note-GrovesBell2004-13" id="mwkg"><span class="mw-reflink-text" id="mwkw"><span class="cite-bracket" id="mwlA">[</span>13<span class="cite-bracket" id="mwlQ">]</span></span></a></sup> A 2013 <a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="//en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Phylogenetic" title="Phylogenetic" class="mw-redirect" id="mwlg">phylogenetic</a> study found that the plains zebra is more closely related to Grévy's zebras than mountain zebras.<sup about="#mwt59" class="mw-ref reference" id="cite_ref-Vilstrup_14-0" rel="dc:references" typeof="mw:Extension/ref" data-mw='{"name":"ref","attrs":{"name":"Vilstrup"},"body":{"id":"mw-reference-text-cite_note-Vilstrup-14"}}'><a href="#cite_note-Vilstrup-14" id="mwlw"><span class="mw-reflink-text" id="mwmA"><span class="cite-bracket" id="mwmQ">[</span>14<span class="cite-bracket" id="mwmg">]</span></span></a></sup> The extinct <a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="//en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Quagga" title="Quagga" id="mwmw">quagga</a> was originally classified as a distinct species.<sup about="#mwt62" class="mw-ref reference" id="cite_ref-15" rel="dc:references" typeof="mw:Extension/ref" data-mw='{"name":"ref","attrs":{},"body":{"id":"mw-reference-text-cite_note-15"}}'><a href="#cite_note-15" id="mwnA"><span class="mw-reflink-text" id="mwnQ"><span class="cite-bracket" id="mwng">[</span>15<span class="cite-bracket" id="mwnw">]</span></span></a></sup> Later genetic studies have placed it as the same species as the plains zebra, either a subspecies or just the southernmost population.<sup about="#mwt65" class="mw-ref reference" id="cite_ref-smithsonian_16-0" rel="dc:references" typeof="mw:Extension/ref" data-mw='{"name":"ref","attrs":{"name":"smithsonian"},"body":{"id":"mw-reference-text-cite_note-smithsonian-16"}}'><a href="#cite_note-smithsonian-16" id="mwoA"><span class="mw-reflink-text" id="mwoQ"><span class="cite-bracket" id="mwog">[</span>16<span class="cite-bracket" id="mwow">]</span></span></a></sup><sup about="#mwt68" class="mw-ref reference" id="cite_ref-PedersenAlbrechtsen2018_17-0" rel="dc:references" typeof="mw:Extension/ref" data-mw='{"name":"ref","attrs":{"name":"PedersenAlbrechtsen2018"},"body":{"id":"mw-reference-text-cite_note-PedersenAlbrechtsen2018-17"}}'><a href="#cite_note-PedersenAlbrechtsen2018-17" id="mwpA"><span class="mw-reflink-text" id="mwpQ"><span class="cite-bracket" id="mwpg">[</span>17<span class="cite-bracket" id="mwpw">]</span></span></a></sup> Molecular evidence supports zebras as a <a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="//en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Monophyletic" title="Monophyletic" class="mw-redirect" id="mwqA">monophyletic</a> <a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="//en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lineage_(evolution)" title="Lineage (evolution)" id="mwqQ">lineage</a>.<sup about="#mwt69" class="mw-ref reference" id="cite_ref-Vilstrup_14-1" rel="dc:references" typeof="mw:Extension/ref" data-mw='{"name":"ref","attrs":{"name":"Vilstrup"}}'><a href="#cite_note-Vilstrup-14" id="mwqg"><span class="mw-reflink-text" id="mwqw"><span class="cite-bracket" id="mwrA">[</span>14<span class="cite-bracket" id="mwrQ">]</span></span></a></sup><sup about="#mwt72" class="mw-ref reference" id="cite_ref-Forstén_18-0" rel="dc:references" typeof="mw:Extension/ref" data-mw='{"name":"ref","attrs":{"name":"Forstén"},"body":{"id":"mw-reference-text-cite_note-Forstén-18"}}'><a href="#cite_note-Forstén-18" id="mwrg"><span class="mw-reflink-text" id="mwrw"><span class="cite-bracket" id="mwsA">[</span>18<span class="cite-bracket" id="mwsQ">]</span></span></a></sup><sup about="#mwt75" class="mw-ref reference" id="cite_ref-19" rel="dc:references" typeof="mw:Extension/ref" data-mw='{"name":"ref","attrs":{},"body":{"id":"mw-reference-text-cite_note-19"}}'><a href="#cite_note-19" id="mwsg"><span class="mw-reflink-text" id="mwsw"><span class="cite-bracket" id="mwtA">[</span>19<span class="cite-bracket" id="mwtQ">]</span></span></a></sup></p><p id="mwtg"><i id="mwtw">Equus</i> originated in North America and direct <a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="//en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Paleogenomics" title="Paleogenomics" id="mwuA">paleogenomic</a> sequencing of a 700,000-year-old middle Pleistocene horse <a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="//en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Metapodial" title="Metapodial" id="mwuQ">metapodial</a> bone from Canada implies a date of 4.07 million years ago (mya) for the most <a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="//en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Recent_common_ancestor" title="Recent common ancestor" class="mw-redirect" id="mwug">recent common ancestor</a> of the equines within a range of 4.0 to 4.5 mya.<sup about="#mwt78" class="mw-ref reference" id="cite_ref-ReferenceA_20-0" rel="dc:references" typeof="mw:Extension/ref" data-mw='{"name":"ref","attrs":{"name":"ReferenceA"},"body":{"id":"mw-reference-text-cite_note-ReferenceA-20"}}'><a href="#cite_note-ReferenceA-20" id="mwuw"><span class="mw-reflink-text" id="mwvA"><span class="cite-bracket" id="mwvQ">[</span>20<span class="cite-bracket" id="mwvg">]</span></span></a></sup> Horses <a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="//en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Split_(phylogenetics)" title="Split (phylogenetics)" id="mwvw">split</a> from asses and zebras around this time and equines colonised Eurasia and Africa around 2.1–3.4 mya. Zebras and asses diverged from each other close to 2 mya. The mountain zebra diverged from the other species around 1.6 mya and the plains and Grévy's zebra split 1.4 mya.<sup about="#mwt81" class="mw-ref reference" id="cite_ref-Jónsson2014_21-0" rel="dc:references" typeof="mw:Extension/ref" data-mw='{"name":"ref","attrs":{"name":"Jónsson2014"},"body":{"id":"mw-reference-text-cite_note-Jónsson2014-21"}}'><a href="#cite_note-Jónsson2014-21" id="mwwA"><span class="mw-reflink-text" id="mwwQ"><span class="cite-bracket" id="mwwg">[</span>21<span class="cite-bracket" id="mwww">]</span></span></a></sup></p><p id="mwxA">A 2017 mitochondrial DNA study placed the Eurasian <i id="mwxQ"><a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="//en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Equus_ovodovi" title="Equus ovodovi" id="mwxg">Equus ovodovi</a></i> and the subgenus <i id="mwxw">Sussemionus</i> lineage as closer to zebras than to asses.<sup about="#mwt84" class="mw-ref reference" id="cite_ref-22" rel="dc:references" typeof="mw:Extension/ref" data-mw='{"name":"ref","attrs":{},"body":{"id":"mw-reference-text-cite_note-22"}}'><a href="#cite_note-22" id="mwyA"><span class="mw-reflink-text" id="mwyQ"><span class="cite-bracket" id="mwyg">[</span>22<span class="cite-bracket" id="mwyw">]</span></span></a></sup> However, other studies disputed this placement, finding the <i id="mwzA">Sussemionus</i> lineage basal to the zebra+asses group, but suggested that the <i id="mwzQ">Sussemionus</i> lineage may have received gene flow from zebras.<sup about="#mwt87" class="mw-ref reference" id="cite_ref-23" rel="dc:references" typeof="mw:Extension/ref" data-mw='{"name":"ref","attrs":{},"body":{"id":"mw-reference-text-cite_note-23"}}'><a href="#cite_note-23" id="mwzg"><span class="mw-reflink-text" id="mwzw"><span class="cite-bracket" id="mw0A">[</span>23<span class="cite-bracket" id="mw0Q">]</span></span></a></sup></p><p id="mw0g">The cladogram of <i id="mw0w">Equus</i> below is based on Vilstrup and colleagues (2013) and Jónsson and colleagues (2014):<sup about="#mwt88" class="mw-ref reference" id="cite_ref-Vilstrup_14-2" rel="dc:references" typeof="mw:Extension/ref" data-mw='{"name":"ref","attrs":{"name":"Vilstrup"}}'><a href="#cite_note-Vilstrup-14" id="mw1A"><span class="mw-reflink-text" id="mw1Q"><span class="cite-bracket" id="mw1g">[</span>14<span class="cite-bracket" id="mw1w">]</span></span></a></sup><sup about="#mwt89" class="mw-ref reference" id="cite_ref-Jónsson2014_21-1" rel="dc:references" typeof="mw:Extension/ref" data-mw='{"name":"ref","attrs":{"name":"Jónsson2014"}}'><a href="#cite_note-Jónsson2014-21" id="mw2A"><span class="mw-reflink-text" id="mw2Q"><span class="cite-bracket" id="mw2g">[</span>21<span class="cite-bracket" id="mw2w">]</span></span></a></sup></p><div class="clade" about="#mwt90" typeof="mw:Transclusion" id="mw3A" data-mw="{&quot;parts&quot;:[{&quot;template&quot;:{&quot;target&quot;:{&quot;wt&quot;:&quot;clade&quot;,&quot;href&quot;:&quot;./Template:Clade&quot;},&quot;params&quot;:{&quot;style&quot;:{&quot;wt&quot;:&quot;font-size:85%; class="mw-file-element"/></a></span></p></td></tr><tr><td class="clade-slabel"></td></tr><tr><td class="clade-label"></td><td rowspan="2" class="clade-leaf"><p><a rel="mw:WikiLink" record</h3></div><figure class="mw-default-size mw-halign-right" typeof="mw:File/Thumb mw:ExpandedAttrs" about="#mwt173" id="mwAbQ" data-mw='{"attribs":[["alt",{"html":"alt= A fossil skull of &lt;i id=\"mwAbM\">Equus mauritanicu&lt;/i> ","txt":" A fossil skull of Equus mauritanicu"}]]}'><a href="//en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Equus_mauritanicus.JPG" class="mw-file-description" id="mwAbU"><img alt=" A fossil skull of Equus mauritanicu" resource="//en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Equus_mauritanicus.JPG" src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/c/ce/Equus_mauritanicus.JPG/250px-Equus_mauritanicus.JPG" decoding="async" data-file-width="3422" data-file-height="1281" data-file-type="bitmap" height="94" width="250" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/c/ce/Equus_mauritanicus.JPG/500px-Equus_mauritanicus.JPG 2x" class="mw-file-element" id="mwAbY"/></a><figcaption id="mwAbc"> Fossil skull of <i id="mwAbg">Equus mauritanicus</i></figcaption></figure><p id="mwAbk">In addition to the three living species, some fossil zebras and relatives have also been identified. <i id="mwAbo">E. oldowayensis</i> is identified from remains in Olduvai Gorge dating to 1.8 mya.<sup about="#mwt174" class="mw-ref reference" id="cite_ref-Churcher2006_36-0" rel="dc:references" typeof="mw:Extension/ref" data-mw='{"name":"ref","attrs":{"name":"Churcher2006"}}'><a href="#cite_note-Churcher2006-36" id="mwAbs"><span class="mw-reflink-text" id="mwAbw"><span class="cite-bracket" id="mwAb0">[</span>36<span class="cite-bracket" id="mwAb4">]</span></span></a></sup> Fossil skulls of <i id="mwAb8">E. mauritanicus</i> from Algeria which date to around 1 mya appears to show affinities with the plains zebra.<sup about="#mwt177" class="mw-ref reference" id="cite_ref-Azzaroli_37-0" rel="dc:references" typeof="mw:Extension/ref" data-mw='{"name":"ref","attrs":{"name":"Azzaroli"},"body":{"id":"mw-reference-text-cite_note-Azzaroli-37"}}'><a href="#cite_note-Azzaroli-37" id="mwAcA"><span class="mw-reflink-text" id="mwAcE"><span class="cite-bracket" id="mwAcI">[</span>37<span class="cite-bracket" id="mwAcM">]</span></span></a></sup><sup about="#mwt180" class="mw-ref reference" id="cite_ref-38" rel="dc:references" typeof="mw:Extension/ref" data-mw='{"name":"ref","attrs":{},"body":{"id":"mw-reference-text-cite_note-38"}}'><a href="#cite_note-38" id="mwAcQ"><span class="mw-reflink-text" id="mwAcU"><span class="cite-bracket" id="mwAcY">[</span>38<span class="cite-bracket" id="mwAcc">]</span></span></a></sup> <i id="mwAcg"><a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="//en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Equus_capensis" title="Equus capensis" id="mwAck">E. capensis</a></i>, known as the Cape zebra, appeared around 2 mya and lived throughout southern and eastern Africa.<sup about="#mwt183" class="mw-ref reference" id="cite_ref-Badenhorst2019_1-1" rel="dc:references" typeof="mw:Extension/ref" data-mw='{"name":"ref","attrs":{"name":"Badenhorst2019"},"body":{"id":"mw-reference-text-cite_note-Badenhorst2019-1"}}'><a href="#cite_note-Badenhorst2019-1" id="mwAco"><span class="mw-reflink-text" id="mwAcs"><span class="cite-bracket" id="mwAcw">[</span>1<span class="cite-bracket" id="mwAc0">]</span></span></a></sup><sup about="#mwt186" class="mw-ref reference" id="cite_ref-Churcher2006_36-1" rel="dc:references" typeof="mw:Extension/ref" data-mw='{"name":"ref","attrs":{"name":"Churcher2006"},"body":{"id":"mw-reference-text-cite_note-Churcher2006-36"}}'><a href="#cite_note-Churcher2006-36" id="mwAc4"><span class="mw-reflink-text" id="mwAc8"><span class="cite-bracket" id="mwAdA">[</span>36<span class="cite-bracket" id="mwAdE">]</span></span></a></sup></p></section><section data-mw-section-id="5" id="mwAdI" aria-labelledby="Hybridisation"><div class="mw-heading mw-heading3"><h3 id="Hybridisation">Hybridisation</h3></div><span class="mw-empty-elt" about="#mwt187" typeof="mw:Transclusion" id="mwAdM" data-mw='{"parts":[{"template":{"target":{"wt":"Main","href":"./Template:Main"},"params":{"1":{"wt":"Zebroid"}},"i":0}}]}'><link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1353705441" about="#mwt188" typeof="mw:Extension/templatestyles" data-mw='{"name":"templatestyles","attrs":{"src":"Module:Hatnote/styles.css"},"body":{"extsrc":""}}'/></span><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable" about="#mwt187" id="mwAdQ">Main article: <a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="//en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Zebroid" title="Zebroid">Zebroid</a></div><p id="mwAdU">Fertile hybrids have been reported in the wild between plains and Grévy's zebra.<sup about="#mwt191" class="mw-ref reference" id="cite_ref-39" rel="dc:references" typeof="mw:Extension/ref" data-mw='{"name":"ref","attrs":{},"body":{"id":"mw-reference-text-cite_note-39"}}'><a href="#cite_note-39" id="mwAdY"><span class="mw-reflink-text" id="mwAdc"><span class="cite-bracket" id="mwAdg">[</span>39<span class="cite-bracket" id="mwAdk">]</span></span></a></sup> Hybridisation has also been recorded between the plains and mountain zebra, though it is possible that these are infertile due to the difference in chromosome numbers between the two species.<sup about="#mwt194" class="mw-ref reference" id="cite_ref-40" rel="dc:references" typeof="mw:Extension/ref" data-mw='{"name":"ref","attrs":{},"body":{"id":"mw-reference-text-cite_note-40"}}'><a href="#cite_note-40" id="mwAdo"><span class="mw-reflink-text" id="mwAds"><span class="cite-bracket" id="mwAdw">[</span>40<span class="cite-bracket" id="mwAd0">]</span></span></a></sup> Captive zebras have been bred with horses and <a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="//en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Donkeys" title="Donkeys" class="mw-redirect" id="mwAd4">donkeys</a>; these are known as <a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="//en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Zebroids" title="Zebroids" class="mw-redirect" id="mwAd8">zebroids</a>. A zorse is a cross between a zebra and a horse; a zonkey, between a zebra and a donkey; and a zoni, between a zebra and a <a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="//en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pony" title="Pony" id="mwAeA">pony</a>. Zebroids are often born sterile with <a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="//en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dwarfism" title="Dwarfism" id="mwAeE">dwarfism</a>.<sup about="#mwt197" class="mw-ref reference" id="cite_ref-41" rel="dc:references" typeof="mw:Extension/ref" data-mw='{"name":"ref","attrs":{},"body":{"id":"mw-reference-text-cite_note-41"}}'><a href="#cite_note-41" id="mwAeI"><span class="mw-reflink-text" id="mwAeM"><span class="cite-bracket" id="mwAeQ">[</span>41<span class="cite-bracket" id="mwAeU">]</span></span></a></sup></p><ul class="gallery mw-gallery-packed" typeof="mw:Extension/gallery" about="#mwt198" id="mwAeY" data-mw='{"name":"gallery","attrs":{"mode":"packed","heights":"150px"},"body":{}}'><li class="gallerybox" style="width: 243.33333333333px;" id="mwAec"><div class="thumb" style="width: 241.33333333333px;" id="mwAeg"><span typeof="mw:File" id="mwAek"><a href="//en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Grévy's_×_Plains_Zebra,_ol_pejeta_imported_from_iNaturalist_photo_401473965_(cropped).jpg" class="mw-file-description" title="Grévy's × plains zebra hybrid, alongside plains zebras" id="mwAeo"><img alt="Grévy's × plains zebra hybrid, alongside plains zebras" decoding="async" data-file-width="1352" data-file-height="842" data-file-type="bitmap" height="150" width="242" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/6/6c/Gr%C3%A9vy%27s_%C3%97_Plains_Zebra%2C_ol_pejeta_imported_from_iNaturalist_photo_401473965_%28cropped%29.jpg/960px-Gr%C3%A9vy%27s_%C3%97_Plains_Zebra%2C_ol_pejeta_imported_from_iNaturalist_photo_401473965_%28cropped%29.jpg 2x" class="mw-file-element" id="mwAes"/></a></span></div><div class="gallerytext" id="mwAew">Grévy's × plains zebra hybrid, alongside plains zebras</div></li><li class="gallerybox" style="width: 272.66666666667px;" id="mwAe0"><div class="thumb" style="width: 270.66666666667px;" id="mwAe4"><span typeof="mw:File" id="mwAe8"><a href="//en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Donkey_×_Plains_Zebra,_Cedarberg_Guest_Farm,_Cedarville,_South_Africa_imported_from_iNaturalist_photo_11786274_(cropped).jpg" class="mw-file-description" title="Donkey × plains zebra hybrid, in South Africa" id="mwAfA"><img alt="Donkey × plains zebra hybrid, in South Africa" resource="//en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Donkey_×_Plains_Zebra,_Cedarberg_Guest_Farm,_Cedarville,_South_Africa_imported_from_iNaturalist_photo_11786274_(cropped).jpg" src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/a/a4/Donkey_%C3%97_Plains_Zebra%2C_Cedarberg_Guest_Farm%2C_Cedarville%2C_South_Africa_imported_from_iNaturalist_photo_11786274_%28cropped%29.jpg/500px-Donkey_%C3%97_Plains_Zebra%2C_Cedarberg_Guest_Farm%2C_Cedarville%2C_South_Africa_imported_from_iNaturalist_photo_11786274_%28cropped%29.jpg" decoding="async" data-file-width="1479" data-file-height="820" data-file-type="bitmap" height="150" width="271" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/a/a4/Donkey_%C3%97_Plains_Zebra%2C_Cedarberg_Guest_Farm%2C_Cedarville%2C_South_Africa_imported_from_iNaturalist_photo_11786274_%28cropped%29.jpg/960px-Donkey_%C3%97_Plains_Zebra%2C_Cedarberg_Guest_Farm%2C_Cedarville%2C_South_Africa_imported_from_iNaturalist_photo_11786274_%28cropped%29.jpg 2x" class="mw-file-element" id="mwAfE"/></a></span></div><div class="gallerytext" id="mwAfI">Donkey × plains zebra hybrid, in South Africa</div></li></ul></section></section><section data-mw-section-id="6" id="mwAfM" aria-labelledby="Characteristics"><div class="mw-heading mw-heading2"><h2 id="Characteristics">Characteristics</h2></div><span class="mw-empty-elt" about="#mwt199" typeof="mw:Transclusion" id="mwAfQ" data-mw='{"parts":[{"template":{"target":{"wt":"Further","href":"./Template:Further"},"params":{"1":{"wt":"Equine anatomy"}},"i":0}}]}'><link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1353705441" about="#mwt200" typeof="mw:Extension/templatestyles" data-mw='{"name":"templatestyles","attrs":{"src":"Module:Hatnote/styles.css"},"body":{"extsrc":""}}'/></span><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable" about="#mwt199" id="mwAfU">Further information: <a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="//en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Equine_anatomy" title="Equine anatomy">Equine anatomy</a></div><figure class="mw-default-size mw-halign-right" typeof="mw:File/Thumb" id="mwAfY"><a href="//en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Equus_grevyi_01.JPG" class="mw-file-description" id="mwAfc"><img alt="Mounted skeleton of a Grévy's zebra Cranium, complete skeleton, left forefoot frontal, left forefoot side" resource="//en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Equus_grevyi_01.JPG" src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/3/3d/Equus_grevyi_01.JPG/250px-Equus_grevyi_01.JPG" decoding="async" data-file-width="5502" data-file-height="2086" data-file-type="bitmap" height="95" width="250" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/3/3d/Equus_grevyi_01.JPG/500px-Equus_grevyi_01.JPG 2x" class="mw-file-element" id="mwAfg"/></a><figcaption id="mwAfk">Skeleton of a Grévy's zebra at the <a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="//en.wikipedia.org/wiki/State_Museum_of_Natural_History_Karlsruhe" title="State Museum of Natural History Karlsruhe" id="mwAfo">State Museum of Natural History Karlsruhe</a></figcaption></figure><p id="mwAfs">As with all wild equines, zebras have barrel-chested bodies with <a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="//en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tail_(horse)" title="Tail (horse)" id="mwAfw">tufted tails</a>, elongated faces and long necks with long, erect <a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="//en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mane_(horse)" title="Mane (horse)" id="mwAf0">manes</a>. Their thin legs are each supported by a spade-shaped toe covered in a hard <a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="//en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Horse_hoof" title="Horse hoof" id="mwAf4">hoof</a>. Their <a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="//en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Horse_teeth" title="Horse teeth" id="mwAf8">dentition</a> is adapted for <a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="//en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Grazing_(behaviour)" title="Grazing (behaviour)" id="mwAgA">grazing</a>; they have large incisors that clip grass blades and rough molars and premolars well suited for grinding. Males have spade-shaped canines, which can be used as weapons in fighting. The eyes of zebras are at the sides and far up the head, which allows them to look over the tall grass while feeding. Their moderately long, erect ears are movable and can locate the source of a sound.<sup about="#mwt201" class="mw-ref reference" id="cite_ref-MacDonald_9-5" rel="dc:references" typeof="mw:Extension/ref" data-mw='{"name":"ref","attrs":{"name":"MacDonald"}}'><a href="#cite_note-MacDonald-9" id="mwAgE"><span class="mw-reflink-text" id="mwAgI"><span class="cite-bracket" id="mwAgM">[</span>9<span class="cite-bracket" id="mwAgQ">]</span></span></a></sup><sup about="#mwt204" class="mw-ref reference" id="cite_ref-Estes_1991_29-1" rel="dc:references" typeof="mw:Extension/ref" data-mw='{"name":"ref","attrs":{"name":"Estes 1991"},"body":{"id":"mw-reference-text-cite_note-Estes_1991-29"}}'><a href="#cite_note-Estes_1991-29" id="mwAgU"><span class="mw-reflink-text" id="mwAgY"><span class="cite-bracket" id="mwAgc">[</span>29<span class="cite-bracket" id="mwAgg">]</span></span></a></sup><sup about="#mwt207" class="mw-ref reference" id="cite_ref-handbook_35-1" rel="dc:references" typeof="mw:Extension/ref" data-mw='{"name":"ref","attrs":{"name":"handbook"},"body":{"id":"mw-reference-text-cite_note-handbook-35"}}'><a href="#cite_note-handbook-35" id="mwAgk"><span class="mw-reflink-text" id="mwAgo"><span class="cite-bracket" id="mwAgs">[</span>35<span class="cite-bracket" id="mwAgw">]</span></span></a></sup></p><p id="mwAg0">Unlike horses, zebras and asses have <a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="//en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chestnut_(horse_anatomy)" title="Chestnut (horse anatomy)" id="mwAg4">chestnut callosities</a> present only on their front legs. In contrast to other living equines, zebras have longer front legs than back legs.<sup about="#mwt208" class="mw-ref reference" id="cite_ref-handbook_35-2" rel="dc:references" typeof="mw:Extension/ref" data-mw='{"name":"ref","attrs":{"name":"handbook"}}'><a href="#cite_note-handbook-35" id="mwAg8"><span class="mw-reflink-text" id="mwAhA"><span class="cite-bracket" id="mwAhE">[</span>35<span class="cite-bracket" id="mwAhI">]</span></span></a></sup> Diagnostic traits of the zebra skull include: its relatively small size with a straight dorsal outline, protruding eye sockets, narrower rostrum, less conspicuous <a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="//en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Postorbital_bar" title="Postorbital bar" id="mwAhM">postorbital bar</a>, separation of the <a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="//en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Glossary_of_mammalian_dental_topography" title="Glossary of mammalian dental topography" id="mwAhQ">metaconid</a> and <a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="//en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Glossary_of_mammalian_dental_topography" title="Glossary of mammalian dental topography" id="mwAhU">metastylid</a> of the tooth by a V-shaped canal and rounded <a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="//en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tooth_enamel" title="Tooth enamel" id="mwAhY">enamel wall</a>.<sup about="#mwt211" class="mw-ref reference" id="cite_ref-42" rel="dc:references" typeof="mw:Extension/ref" data-mw='{"name":"ref","attrs":{},"body":{"id":"mw-reference-text-cite_note-42"}}'><a href="#cite_note-42" id="mwAhc"><span class="mw-reflink-text" id="mwAhg"><span class="cite-bracket" id="mwAhk">[</span>42<span class="cite-bracket" id="mwAho">]</span></span></a></sup></p><section data-mw-section-id="7" id="mwAhs" aria-labelledby="Stripes"><div class="mw-heading mw-heading3"><h3 id="Stripes">Stripes</h3></div><span class="mw-empty-elt" about="#mwt212" typeof="mw:Transclusion" id="mwAhw" data-mw='{"parts":[{"template":{"target":{"wt":"Redirect","href":"./Template:Redirect"},"params":{"1":{"wt":"Zebra stripes"},"2":{"wt":""},"3":{"wt":"Zebra stripes (disambiguation)"}},"i":0}}]}'><link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1353705441" about="#mwt213" typeof="mw:Extension/templatestyles" data-mw='{"name":"templatestyles","attrs":{"src":"Module:Hatnote/styles.css"},"body":{"extsrc":""}}'/></span><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable" about="#mwt212" id="mwAh0">"Zebra stripes" redirects here. For other uses, see <a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="//en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Zebra_stripes_(disambiguation)" title="Zebra stripes (disambiguation)" class="mw-disambig">Zebra stripes (disambiguation)</a>.</div><figure class="mw-default-size mw-halign-left" typeof="mw:File/Thumb" id="mwAh4"><a href="//en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Zebra_species_(ENG).png" class="mw-file-description" id="mwAh8"><img alt="An illustration showing the three living zebra species" resource="//en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Zebra_species_(ENG).png" src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/6/6c/Zebra_species_%28ENG%29.png/250px-Zebra_species_%28ENG%29.png" decoding="async" data-file-width="2156" data-file-height="1916" data-file-type="bitmap" height="222" width="250" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/6/6c/Zebra_species_%28ENG%29.png/500px-Zebra_species_%28ENG%29.png 2x" class="mw-file-element" id="mwAiA"/></a><figcaption id="mwAiE">Comparative illustration of living zebra species</figcaption></figure><p id="mwAiI">Zebras are easily recognised by their bold black-and-white striping patterns. The <a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="//en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Animal_coat" title="Animal coat" id="mwAiM">coat</a> appears to be white with black stripes, as indicated by the belly and legs when unstriped, but the skin is black.<sup about="#mwt217" class="mw-ref reference" id="cite_ref-Bard1977_43-0" rel="dc:references" typeof="mw:Extension/ref" data-mw='{"name":"ref","attrs":{"name":"Bard1977"}}'><a href="#cite_note-Bard1977-43" id="mwAiQ"><span class="mw-reflink-text" id="mwAiU"><span class="cite-bracket" id="mwAiY">[</span>43<span class="cite-bracket" id="mwAic">]</span></span></a></sup><sup about="#mwt220" class="mw-ref reference" id="cite_ref-44" rel="dc:references" typeof="mw:Extension/ref" data-mw='{"name":"ref","attrs":{},"body":{"id":"mw-reference-text-cite_note-44"}}'><a href="#cite_note-44" id="mwAig"><span class="mw-reflink-text" id="mwAik"><span class="cite-bracket" id="mwAio">[</span>44<span class="cite-bracket" id="mwAis">]</span></span></a></sup><sup about="#mwt214" class="mw-ref reference" id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTECaro201614–15_45-0" rel="dc:references" typeof="mw:Transclusion mw:Extension/ref" data-mw='{"name":"ref","attrs":{"group":"","name":"FOOTNOTECaro201614–15"},"body":{"id":"mw-reference-text-cite_note-FOOTNOTECaro201614–15-45"},"parts":[{"template":{"target":{"wt":"sfn","href":"./Template:Sfn"},"params":{"1":{"wt":"Caro"},"2":{"wt":"2016"},"pp":{"wt":"14–15"}},"i":0}}]}'><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTECaro201614–15-45" id="mwAiw"><span class="mw-reflink-text" id="mwAi0"><span class="cite-bracket" id="mwAi4">[</span>45<span class="cite-bracket" id="mwAi8">]</span></span></a></sup> Young or <a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="//en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Foal" title="Foal" id="mwAjA">foals</a> are born with brown and white coats, and the brown darkens with age.<sup about="#mwt221" class="mw-ref reference" id="cite_ref-Grub_1981_28-1" rel="dc:references" typeof="mw:Extension/ref" data-mw='{"name":"ref","attrs":{"name":"Grub 1981"}}'><a href="#cite_note-Grub_1981-28" id="mwAjE"><span class="mw-reflink-text" id="mwAjI"><span class="cite-bracket" id="mwAjM">[</span>28<span class="cite-bracket" id="mwAjQ">]</span></span></a></sup><sup about="#mwt222" class="mw-ref reference" id="cite_ref-Churcher_1993_25-3" rel="dc:references" typeof="mw:Extension/ref" data-mw='{"name":"ref","attrs":{"name":"Churcher 1993"}}'><a href="#cite_note-Churcher_1993-25" id="mwAjU"><span class="mw-reflink-text" id="mwAjY"><span class="cite-bracket" id="mwAjc">[</span>25<span class="cite-bracket" id="mwAjg">]</span></span></a></sup> A <a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="//en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Primitive_markings#Dorsal_stripe" title="Primitive markings" id="mwAjk">dorsal stripe</a> acts as the backbone for vertical stripes along the sides, from the head to the rump. On the snout they curve toward the nostrils, while the stripes above the front legs split into two branches. On the rump, they develop into species-specific patterns. The stripes on the legs, ears and tail are separate and horizontal.<sup about="#mwt223" class="mw-ref reference" id="cite_ref-Bard1977_43-1" rel="dc:references" typeof="mw:Extension/ref" data-mw='{"name":"ref","attrs":{"name":"Bard1977"}}'><a href="#cite_note-Bard1977-43" id="mwAjo"><span class="mw-reflink-text" id="mwAjs"><span class="cite-bracket" id="mwAjw">[</span>43<span class="cite-bracket" id="mwAj0">]</span></span></a></sup></p><p id="mwAj4">Striping patterns are unique to an individual and heritable.<sup about="#mwt224" class="mw-ref reference" id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTECaro20167,_19_46-0" rel="dc:references" typeof="mw:Transclusion mw:Extension/ref" data-mw='{"name":"ref","attrs":{"group":"","name":"FOOTNOTECaro20167, 19"},"body":{"id":"mw-reference-text-cite_note-FOOTNOTECaro20167,_19-46"},"parts":[{"template":{"target":{"wt":"sfn","href":"./Template:Sfn"},"params":{"1":{"wt":"Caro"},"2":{"wt":"2016"},"pp":{"wt":"7, 19"}},"i":0}}]}'><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTECaro20167,_19-46" id="mwAj8"><span class="mw-reflink-text" id="mwAkA"><span class="cite-bracket" id="mwAkE">[</span>46<span class="cite-bracket" id="mwAkI">]</span></span></a></sup> During <a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="//en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Embryonic_development" title="Embryonic development" class="mw-redirect" id="mwAkM">embryonic development</a>, the stripes appear at eight months, but the patterns may be determined at three to five weeks. For each species there is a point in embryonic development where the stripes are perpendicular to the dorsal line and spaced <span about="#mwt227" typeof="mw:Transclusion" id="mwAkQ" data-mw='{"parts":[{"template":{"target":{"wt":"convert","href":"./Template:Convert"},"params":{"1":{"wt":"0.4"},"2":{"wt":"mm"},"3":{"wt":"in"},"abbr":{"wt":"on"}},"i":0}}]}'>0.4</span><span typeof="mw:Entity" about="#mwt227"> </span><span about="#mwt227">mm (0.016</span><span typeof="mw:Entity" about="#mwt227"> </span><span about="#mwt227">in)</span> apart. However, this happens at three weeks of development for the plains zebra, four weeks for the mountain zebra, and five for Grévy's zebra. The difference in timing is thought to be responsible for the differences in the striping patterns of the different species.<sup about="#mwt230" class="mw-ref reference" id="cite_ref-Bard1977_43-2" rel="dc:references" typeof="mw:Extension/ref" data-mw='{"name":"ref","attrs":{"name":"Bard1977"},"body":{"id":"mw-reference-text-cite_note-Bard1977-43"}}'><a href="#cite_note-Bard1977-43" id="mwAkU"><span class="mw-reflink-text" id="mwAkY"><span class="cite-bracket" id="mwAkc">[</span>43<span class="cite-bracket" id="mwAkg">]</span></span></a></sup></p><p id="mwAkk">Various abnormalities of the patterns have been documented in plains zebras. In "<a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="//en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Melanistic" title="Melanistic" class="mw-redirect" id="mwAko">melanistic</a>" zebras, dark stripes are highly concentrated on the torso but the legs are whiter. "Spotted" individuals have broken up black stripes around the dorsal area.<sup about="#mwt236" class="mw-ref reference" id="cite_ref-Larison2020_47-0" rel="dc:references" typeof="mw:Extension/ref" data-mw='{"name":"ref","attrs":{"name":"Larison2020"},"body":{"id":"mw-reference-text-cite_note-Larison2020-47"}}'><a href="#cite_note-Larison2020-47" id="mwAks"><span class="mw-reflink-text" id="mwAkw"><span class="cite-bracket" id="mwAk0">[</span>47<span class="cite-bracket" id="mwAk4">]</span></span></a></sup> There have even been <a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="//en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Polymorphism_(biology)" title="Polymorphism (biology)" id="mwAk8">morphs</a> with white spots on dark backgrounds.<sup about="#mwt231" class="mw-ref reference" id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTECaro201620_48-0" rel="dc:references" typeof="mw:Transclusion mw:Extension/ref" data-mw='{"name":"ref","attrs":{"group":"","name":"FOOTNOTECaro201620"},"body":{"id":"mw-reference-text-cite_note-FOOTNOTECaro201620-48"},"parts":[{"template":{"target":{"wt":"sfn","href":"./Template:Sfn"},"params":{"1":{"wt":"Caro"},"2":{"wt":"2016"},"p":{"wt":"20"}},"i":0}}]}'><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTECaro201620-48" id="mwAlA"><span class="mw-reflink-text" id="mwAlE"><span class="cite-bracket" id="mwAlI">[</span>48<span class="cite-bracket" id="mwAlM">]</span></span></a></sup> Striping abnormalities have been linked to <a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="//en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Inbreeding" title="Inbreeding" id="mwAlQ">inbreeding</a>.<sup about="#mwt237" class="mw-ref reference" id="cite_ref-Larison2020_47-1" rel="dc:references" typeof="mw:Extension/ref" data-mw='{"name":"ref","attrs":{"name":"Larison2020"}}'><a href="#cite_note-Larison2020-47" id="mwAlU"><span class="mw-reflink-text" id="mwAlY"><span class="cite-bracket" id="mwAlc">[</span>47<span class="cite-bracket" id="mwAlg">]</span></span></a></sup> <a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="//en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Albino" title="Albino" class="mw-redirect" id="mwAlk">Albino</a> zebras have been recorded in the forests of <a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="//en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mount_Kenya" title="Mount Kenya" id="mwAlo">Mount Kenya</a>, with the dark stripes being blonde.<sup about="#mwt240" class="mw-ref reference" id="cite_ref-49" rel="dc:references" typeof="mw:Extension/ref" data-mw='{"name":"ref","attrs":{},"body":{"id":"mw-reference-text-cite_note-49"}}'><a href="#cite_note-49" id="mwAls"><span class="mw-reflink-text" id="mwAlw"><span class="cite-bracket" id="mwAl0">[</span>49<span class="cite-bracket" id="mwAl4">]</span></span></a></sup> The quagga had brown and white stripes on the head and neck, brown upper parts and a white belly, tail and legs.<sup about="#mwt243" class="mw-ref reference" id="cite_ref-Walker_50-0" rel="dc:references" typeof="mw:Extension/ref" data-mw='{"name":"ref","attrs":{"name":"Walker"},"body":{"id":"mw-reference-text-cite_note-Walker-50"}}'><a href="#cite_note-Walker-50" id="mwAl8"><span class="mw-reflink-text" id="mwAmA"><span class="cite-bracket" id="mwAmE">[</span>50<span class="cite-bracket" id="mwAmI">]</span></span></a></sup></p><section data-mw-section-id="8" id="mwAmM" aria-labelledby="Function"><div class="mw-heading mw-heading4"><h4 id="Function">Function</h4></div><p id="mwAmQ">The function of stripes in zebras has been discussed among biologists since at least the 19th century.<sup about="#mwt244" class="mw-ref reference" id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTECaro20161_51-0" rel="dc:references" typeof="mw:Transclusion mw:Extension/ref" data-mw='{"name":"ref","attrs":{"group":"","name":"FOOTNOTECaro20161"},"body":{"id":"mw-reference-text-cite_note-FOOTNOTECaro20161-51"},"parts":[{"template":{"target":{"wt":"sfn","href":"./Template:Sfn"},"params":{"1":{"wt":"Caro"},"2":{"wt":"2016"},"p":{"wt":"1"}},"i":0}}]}'><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTECaro20161-51" id="mwAmU"><span class="mw-reflink-text" id="mwAmY"><span class="cite-bracket" id="mwAmc">[</span>51<span class="cite-bracket" id="mwAmg">]</span></span></a></sup> Popular hypotheses include the following:</p><ul id="mwAmk"><li id="mwAmo">The <b id="mwAms"><a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="//en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Crypsis" title="Crypsis" id="mwAmw">crypsis</a> hypothesis</b> suggests that the stripes allow the animal to blend in with its environment or <a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="//en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Disruptive_coloration" title="Disruptive coloration" id="mwAm0">break up its outline</a>. This was the earliest hypothesis and proponents argued that the stripes were particularly suited for camouflage in tall grassland and woodland habitat. <a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="//en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alfred_Wallace" title="Alfred Wallace" class="mw-redirect" id="mwAm4">Alfred Wallace</a> also wrote in 1896 that stripes make zebras less noticeable at night. Biologist <a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="//en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tim_Caro" title="Tim Caro" id="mwAm8">Tim Caro</a> notes that zebras graze in open habitat and do not behave cryptically, being noisy, fast, and social and do not freeze when a predator is near. In addition, the camouflaging stripes of woodland living ungulates like <a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="//en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bongo_(antelope)" title="Bongo (antelope)" id="mwAnA">bongos</a> and <a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="//en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cape_bushbuck" title="Cape bushbuck" id="mwAnE">bushbucks</a> are much less vivid with less contrast with the background colour.<sup about="#mwt247" class="mw-ref reference" id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTECaro20162–3,_23,_48,_50_52-0" rel="dc:references" typeof="mw:Transclusion mw:Extension/ref" data-mw='{"name":"ref","attrs":{"group":"","name":"FOOTNOTECaro20162–3, 23, 48, 50"},"body":{"id":"mw-reference-text-cite_note-FOOTNOTECaro20162–3,_23,_48,_50-52"},"parts":[{"template":{"target":{"wt":"sfn","href":"./Template:Sfn"},"params":{"1":{"wt":"Caro"},"2":{"wt":"2016"},"pp":{"wt":"2–3, 23, 48, 50"}},"i":0}}]}'><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTECaro20162–3,_23,_48,_50-52" id="mwAnI"><span class="mw-reflink-text" id="mwAnM"><span class="cite-bracket" id="mwAnQ">[</span>52<span class="cite-bracket" id="mwAnU">]</span></span></a></sup> A 1987 <a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="//en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fourier_analysis" title="Fourier analysis" id="mwAnY">Fourier analysis</a> study concluded that the patterns of zebra stripes do not line up with their environment,<sup about="#mwt252" class="mw-ref reference" id="cite_ref-53" rel="dc:references" typeof="mw:Extension/ref" data-mw='{"name":"ref","attrs":{},"body":{"id":"mw-reference-text-cite_note-53"}}'><a href="#cite_note-53" id="mwAnc"><span class="mw-reflink-text" id="mwAng"><span class="cite-bracket" id="mwAnk">[</span>53<span class="cite-bracket" id="mwAno">]</span></span></a></sup> while a 2014 study of wild equine species and subspecies could not find any correlations between striping patterns and woodland habitats.<sup about="#mwt253" class="mw-ref reference" id="cite_ref-Caro_54-0" rel="dc:references" typeof="mw:Extension/ref" data-mw='{"name":"ref","attrs":{"name":"Caro"}}'><a href="#cite_note-Caro-54" id="mwAns"><span class="mw-reflink-text" id="mwAnw"><span class="cite-bracket" id="mwAn0">[</span>54<span class="cite-bracket" id="mwAn4">]</span></span></a></sup> Melin and colleagues (2016) found that <a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="//en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lion" title="Lion" id="mwAn8">lions</a> and hyenas do not appear to perceive the stripes when they are a certain distance away at daytime or nighttime, thus making the stripes useless in blending in except when the predators are close enough by which they could smell or hear their target. They also found that the stripes do not make the zebra less noticeable than solidly coloured herbivores on the open plains. They suggested that stripes may give zebras an advantage in woodlands, as the dark stripes could line up with the outlines of tree branches and other vegetation.<sup about="#mwt256" class="mw-ref reference" id="cite_ref-55" rel="dc:references" typeof="mw:Extension/ref" data-mw='{"name":"ref","attrs":{},"body":{"id":"mw-reference-text-cite_note-55"}}'><a href="#cite_note-55" id="mwAoA"><span class="mw-reflink-text" id="mwAoE"><span class="cite-bracket" id="mwAoI">[</span>55<span class="cite-bracket" id="mwAoM">]</span></span></a></sup></li></ul><figure class="mw-default-size mw-halign-right" typeof="mw:File/Thumb" id="mwAoQ"><a href="//en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Mountain_zebra_stripes.jpg" class="mw-file-description" id="mwAoU"><img alt="Closeup shot of mountain zebra stripes" resource="//en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Mountain_zebra_stripes.jpg" src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/a/ae/Mountain_zebra_stripes.jpg/250px-Mountain_zebra_stripes.jpg" decoding="async" data-file-width="876" data-file-height="597" data-file-type="bitmap" height="170" width="250" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/a/ae/Mountain_zebra_stripes.jpg/500px-Mountain_zebra_stripes.jpg 2x" class="mw-file-element" id="mwAoY"/></a><figcaption id="mwAoc">Closeup of mountain zebra stripes</figcaption></figure><ul id="mwAog"><li id="mwAok">The <b id="mwAoo">confusion hypothesis</b> states that the stripes confuse predators, be it by: making it harder to distinguish individuals in a group as well as determining the number of zebras in a group; making it difficult to determine an individual's outline when the group runs away; reducing a predator's ability to keep track of a target during a chase; <a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="//en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dazzle_camouflage" title="Dazzle camouflage" id="mwAos">dazzling</a> an assailant so they have difficulty making contact; or making it difficult for a predator to deduce the zebra's size, speed and direction via <a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="//en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Motion_dazzle" title="Motion dazzle" class="mw-redirect" id="mwAow">motion dazzle</a>. This theory has been proposed by several biologists since at least the 1970s.<sup about="#mwt257" class="mw-ref reference" id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTECaro201672–81,_86_56-0" rel="dc:references" typeof="mw:Transclusion mw:Extension/ref" data-mw='{"name":"ref","attrs":{"group":"","name":"FOOTNOTECaro201672–81, 86"},"body":{"id":"mw-reference-text-cite_note-FOOTNOTECaro201672–81,_86-56"},"parts":[{"template":{"target":{"wt":"sfn","href":"./Template:Sfn"},"params":{"1":{"wt":"Caro"},"2":{"wt":"2016"},"pp":{"wt":"72–81, 86"}},"i":0}}]}'><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTECaro201672–81,_86-56" id="mwAo0"><span class="mw-reflink-text" id="mwAo4"><span class="cite-bracket" id="mwAo8">[</span>56<span class="cite-bracket" id="mwApA">]</span></span></a></sup> A 2014 computer study of zebra stripes found that they may create a <a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="//en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wagon-wheel_effect" title="Wagon-wheel effect" id="mwApE">wagon-wheel effect</a> and/or <a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="//en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Barber_pole_illusion" title="Barber pole illusion" class="mw-redirect" id="mwApI">barber pole illusion</a> when in motion. The researchers concluded that this could be used against mammalian predators or biting flies.<sup about="#mwt265" class="mw-ref reference" id="cite_ref-How2014_57-0" rel="dc:references" typeof="mw:Extension/ref" data-mw='{"name":"ref","attrs":{"name":"How2014"},"body":{"id":"mw-reference-text-cite_note-How2014-57"}}'><a href="#cite_note-How2014-57" id="mwApM"><span class="mw-reflink-text" id="mwApQ"><span class="cite-bracket" id="mwApU">[</span>57<span class="cite-bracket" id="mwApY">]</span></span></a></sup> The use of the stripes for confusing mammalian predators has been questioned. Caro suggests that the stripes of zebras could make groups seem smaller, and thus more likely to be attacked. Zebras also tend to scatter when fleeing from attackers and thus the stripes could not break up an individual's outline. Lions, in particular, appear to have no difficulty targeting and catching zebras when they get close and take them by ambush.<sup about="#mwt260" class="mw-ref reference" id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTECaro201680,_92_58-0" rel="dc:references" typeof="mw:Transclusion mw:Extension/ref" data-mw='{"name":"ref","attrs":{"group":"","name":"FOOTNOTECaro201680, 92"},"body":{"id":"mw-reference-text-cite_note-FOOTNOTECaro201680,_92-58"},"parts":[{"template":{"target":{"wt":"sfn","href":"./Template:Sfn"},"params":{"1":{"wt":"Caro"},"2":{"wt":"2016"},"pp":{"wt":"80, 92"}},"i":0}}]}'><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTECaro201680,_92-58" id="mwApc"><span class="mw-reflink-text" id="mwApg"><span class="cite-bracket" id="mwApk">[</span>58<span class="cite-bracket" id="mwApo">]</span></span></a></sup> In addition, no correlations have been found between the number of stripes and populations of mammal predators.<sup about="#mwt266" class="mw-ref reference" id="cite_ref-Caro_54-1" rel="dc:references" typeof="mw:Extension/ref" data-mw='{"name":"ref","attrs":{"name":"Caro"}}'><a href="#cite_note-Caro-54" id="mwAps"><span class="mw-reflink-text" id="mwApw"><span class="cite-bracket" id="mwAp0">[</span>54<span class="cite-bracket" id="mwAp4">]</span></span></a></sup> Hughes and colleagues (2021) disputed the idea of motion dazzle and concluded that moving objects that are solidly grey or have less contrasted patterns are actually more likely to escape being caught.<sup about="#mwt269" class="mw-ref reference" id="cite_ref-59" rel="dc:references" typeof="mw:Extension/ref" data-mw='{"name":"ref","attrs":{},"body":{"id":"mw-reference-text-cite_note-59"}}'><a href="#cite_note-59" id="mwAp8"><span class="mw-reflink-text" id="mwAqA"><span class="cite-bracket" id="mwAqE">[</span>59<span class="cite-bracket" id="mwAqI">]</span></span></a></sup></li><li id="mwAqM">The <b id="mwAqQ"><a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="//en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Aposematism" title="Aposematism" id="mwAqU">aposematic</a> hypothesis</b> suggests that the stripes serve as warning colouration. This hypothesis was first suggested by Wallace in 1867 and discussed in more detail by <a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="//en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Edward_Bagnall_Poulton" title="Edward Bagnall Poulton" id="mwAqY">Edward Bagnall Poulton</a> in 1890. As with known aposematic mammals, zebras are recognizable up close, live in more open environments, have a high risk of predation and do not hide or act inconspicuous. However, Caro notes that stripes do not work on lions because they frequently prey on zebras, though they may work on smaller predators, and zebras are not slow-moving enough to need to ward off threats. In addition, zebras do not possess adequate defenses to back up the warning pattern.<sup about="#mwt270" class="mw-ref reference" id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTECaro201655,_57–58,_68_60-0" rel="dc:references" typeof="mw:Transclusion mw:Extension/ref" data-mw='{"name":"ref","attrs":{"group":"","name":"FOOTNOTECaro201655, 57–58, 68"},"body":{"id":"mw-reference-text-cite_note-FOOTNOTECaro201655,_57–58,_68-60"},"parts":[{"template":{"target":{"wt":"sfn","href":"./Template:Sfn"},"params":{"1":{"wt":"Caro"},"2":{"wt":"2016"},"pp":{"wt":"55, 57–58, 68"}},"i":0}}]}'><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTECaro201655,_57–58,_68-60" id="mwAqc"><span class="mw-reflink-text" id="mwAqg"><span class="cite-bracket" id="mwAqk">[</span>60<span class="cite-bracket" id="mwAqo">]</span></span></a></sup></li><li id="mwAqs">The <b id="mwAqw">social function hypothesis</b> states that stripes serve a role in intraspecific or individual recognition, social bonding, mutual grooming or a signal of <a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="//en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fitness_(biology)" title="Fitness (biology)" id="mwAq0">fitness</a>. <a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="//en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Charles_Darwin" title="Charles Darwin" id="mwAq4">Charles Darwin</a> wrote in 1871<sup about="#mwt273" class="mw-ref reference" id="cite_ref-61" rel="dc:references" typeof="mw:Transclusion mw:Extension/ref" data-mw='{"name":"ref","attrs":{"group":"lower-alpha"},"body":{"id":"mw-reference-text-cite_note-61"},"parts":[{"template":{"target":{"wt":"efn","href":"./Template:Efn"},"params":{"1":{"wt":"The cited source cites the 1896 edition of Darwin&apos;s 1871 book &apos;&apos;[[The Descent of Man, and Selection in Relation to Sex]]&apos;&apos;."}},"i":0}}]}'><a href="#cite_note-61" data-mw-group="lower-alpha" id="mwAq8"><span class="mw-reflink-text" id="mwArA"><span class="cite-bracket" id="mwArE">[</span>a<span class="cite-bracket" id="mwArI">]</span></span></a></sup> that "a female zebra would not admit the addresses of a male ass until he was painted so as to resemble a zebra" while Wallace stated in 1871 that: "The stripes therefore may be of use by enabling stragglers to distinguish their fellows at a distance". Regarding species and individual identification, Caro notes that zebra species have limited range overlap with each other and horses can recognise each other using visual communication.<sup about="#mwt276" class="mw-ref reference" id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTECaro20166–7,_139–148,_150_62-0" rel="dc:references" typeof="mw:Transclusion mw:Extension/ref" data-mw='{"name":"ref","attrs":{"group":"","name":"FOOTNOTECaro20166–7, 139–148, 150"},"body":{"id":"mw-reference-text-cite_note-FOOTNOTECaro20166–7,_139–148,_150-62"},"parts":[{"template":{"target":{"wt":"sfn","href":"./Template:Sfn"},"params":{"1":{"wt":"Caro"},"2":{"wt":"2016"},"pp":{"wt":"6–7, 139–148, 150"}},"i":0}}]}'><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTECaro20166–7,_139–148,_150-62" id="mwArM"><span class="mw-reflink-text" id="mwArQ"><span class="cite-bracket" id="mwArU">[</span>61<span class="cite-bracket" id="mwArY">]</span></span></a></sup> In addition, no correlation has been found between striping and social behaviour or group numbers among equines,<sup about="#mwt282" class="mw-ref reference" id="cite_ref-Caro_54-2" rel="dc:references" typeof="mw:Extension/ref" data-mw='{"name":"ref","attrs":{"name":"Caro"}}'><a href="#cite_note-Caro-54" id="mwArc"><span class="mw-reflink-text" id="mwArg"><span class="cite-bracket" id="mwArk">[</span>54<span class="cite-bracket" id="mwAro">]</span></span></a></sup> and no link has been found between fitness and striping.<sup about="#mwt279" class="mw-ref reference" id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTECaro2016150_63-0" rel="dc:references" typeof="mw:Transclusion mw:Extension/ref" data-mw='{"name":"ref","attrs":{"group":"","name":"FOOTNOTECaro2016150"},"body":{"id":"mw-reference-text-cite_note-FOOTNOTECaro2016150-63"},"parts":[{"template":{"target":{"wt":"sfn","href":"./Template:Sfn"},"params":{"1":{"wt":"Caro"},"2":{"wt":"2016"},"p":{"wt":"150"}},"i":0}}]}'><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTECaro2016150-63" id="mwArs"><span class="mw-reflink-text" id="mwArw"><span class="cite-bracket" id="mwAr0">[</span>62<span class="cite-bracket" id="mwAr4">]</span></span></a></sup></li><li id="mwAr8">The <b id="mwAsA"><a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="//en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Thermoregulation" title="Thermoregulation" id="mwAsE">thermoregulatory</a> hypothesis</b> suggests that stripes help to control a zebra's body temperature. In 1971, biologist H. A. Baldwin noted that heat would be absorbed by the black stripes and reflected by the white ones and in 1990, zoologist <a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="//en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Desmond_Morris" title="Desmond Morris" id="mwAsI">Desmond Morris</a> suggested that the stripes create cooling <a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="//en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Convection_current" title="Convection current" class="mw-redirect" id="mwAsM">convection currents</a>.<sup about="#mwt283" class="mw-ref reference" id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTECaro20167_64-0" rel="dc:references" typeof="mw:Transclusion mw:Extension/ref" data-mw='{"name":"ref","attrs":{"group":"","name":"FOOTNOTECaro20167"},"body":{"id":"mw-reference-text-cite_note-FOOTNOTECaro20167-64"},"parts":[{"template":{"target":{"wt":"sfn","href":"./Template:Sfn"},"params":{"1":{"wt":"Caro"},"2":{"wt":"2016"},"p":{"wt":"7"}},"i":0}}]}'><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTECaro20167-64" id="mwAsQ"><span class="mw-reflink-text" id="mwAsU"><span class="cite-bracket" id="mwAsY">[</span>63<span class="cite-bracket" id="mwAsc">]</span></span></a></sup> A 2019 study supported this, finding that where the faster air currents of the warmer black stripes meet those of the white, <a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="//en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vortex" title="Vortex" id="mwAsg">air swirls</a> form. The researchers also concluded that during the hottest times of the day, zebras <a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="//en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hackles" title="Hackles" id="mwAsk">erect</a> their black hair to release heat from the skin and flatten it again when it gets cooler.<sup about="#mwt291" class="mw-ref reference" id="cite_ref-65" rel="dc:references" typeof="mw:Extension/ref" data-mw='{"name":"ref","attrs":{},"body":{"id":"mw-reference-text-cite_note-65"}}'><a href="#cite_note-65" id="mwAso"><span class="mw-reflink-text" id="mwAss"><span class="cite-bracket" id="mwAsw">[</span>64<span class="cite-bracket" id="mwAs0">]</span></span></a></sup> Larison and colleagues (2015) determined that environmental temperature is a strong predictor for zebra striping patterns.<sup about="#mwt295" class="mw-ref reference" id="cite_ref-66" rel="dc:references" typeof="mw:Extension/ref" data-mw='{"name":"ref","attrs":{},"body":{"id":"mw-reference-text-cite_note-66"}}'><a href="#cite_note-66" id="mwAs4"><span class="mw-reflink-text" id="mwAs8"><span class="cite-bracket" id="mwAtA">[</span>65<span class="cite-bracket" id="mwAtE">]</span></span></a></sup> Others have found no evidence that zebras have lower body temperatures than other ungulates whose habitat they share, or that striping correlates with temperature.<sup about="#mwt286" class="mw-ref reference" id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTECaro2016158–161_67-0" rel="dc:references" typeof="mw:Transclusion mw:Extension/ref" data-mw='{"name":"ref","attrs":{"group":"","name":"FOOTNOTECaro2016158–161"},"body":{"id":"mw-reference-text-cite_note-FOOTNOTECaro2016158–161-67"},"parts":[{"template":{"target":{"wt":"sfn","href":"./Template:Sfn"},"params":{"1":{"wt":"Caro"},"2":{"wt":"2016"},"pp":{"wt":"158–161"}},"i":0}}]}'><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTECaro2016158–161-67" id="mwAtI"><span class="mw-reflink-text" id="mwAtM"><span class="cite-bracket" id="mwAtQ">[</span>66<span class="cite-bracket" id="mwAtU">]</span></span></a></sup><sup about="#mwt296" class="mw-ref reference" id="cite_ref-Caro_54-3" rel="dc:references" typeof="mw:Extension/ref" data-mw='{"name":"ref","attrs":{"name":"Caro"}}'><a href="#cite_note-Caro-54" id="mwAtY"><span class="mw-reflink-text" id="mwAtc"><span class="cite-bracket" id="mwAtg">[</span>54<span class="cite-bracket" id="mwAtk">]</span></span></a></sup> A 2018 experimental study which dressed water-filled metal barrels in horse, zebra and cattle hides concluded that zebra stripes had no effect on thermoregulation.<sup about="#mwt299" class="mw-ref reference" id="cite_ref-68" rel="dc:references" typeof="mw:Extension/ref" data-mw='{"name":"ref","attrs":{},"body":{"id":"mw-reference-text-cite_note-68"}}'><a href="#cite_note-68" id="mwAto"><span class="mw-reflink-text" id="mwAts"><span class="cite-bracket" id="mwAtw">[</span>67<span class="cite-bracket" id="mwAt0">]</span></span></a></sup></li></ul><figure class="mw-default-size mw-halign-right" typeof="mw:File/Thumb" id="mwAt4"><a href="//en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Journal.pone.0210831.g001.png" class="mw-file-description" id="mwAt8"><img alt="Comparison of horse fly flight patterns on horses and zebras" resource="//en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Journal.pone.0210831.g001.png" src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/d/d9/Journal.pone.0210831.g001.png/250px-Journal.pone.0210831.g001.png" decoding="async" data-file-width="2904" data-file-height="3252" data-file-type="bitmap" height="280" width="250" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/d/d9/Journal.pone.0210831.g001.png/500px-Journal.pone.0210831.g001.png 2x" class="mw-file-element" id="mwAuA"/></a><figcaption id="mwAuE">Comparison of flight patterns and contact/landings of horse flies around domestic horses (a-c) and plains zebras (d-f).<sup about="#mwt300" class="mw-ref reference" id="cite_ref-Caro2_69-0" rel="dc:references" typeof="mw:Extension/ref" data-mw='{"name":"ref","attrs":{"name":"Caro2"}}'><a href="#cite_note-Caro2-69" id="mwAuI"><span class="mw-reflink-text" id="mwAuM"><span class="cite-bracket" id="mwAuQ">[</span>68<span class="cite-bracket" id="mwAuU">]</span></span></a></sup></figcaption></figure><ul id="mwAuY"><li id="mwAuc">The <b id="mwAug">fly protection hypothesis</b> holds that the stripes deter <a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="//en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Blood-sucking" title="Blood-sucking" class="mw-redirect" id="mwAuk">blood-sucking</a> flies. <a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="//en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Horse_fly" title="Horse fly" class="mw-redirect" id="mwAuo">Horse flies</a>, in particular, spread diseases that are lethal to equines such as <a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="//en.wikipedia.org/wiki/African_horse_sickness" title="African horse sickness" id="mwAus">African horse sickness</a>, <a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="//en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Equine_influenza" title="Equine influenza" id="mwAuw">equine influenza</a>, <a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="//en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Equine_infectious_anemia" title="Equine infectious anemia" id="mwAu0">equine infectious anemia</a> and <a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="//en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Trypanosomiasis" title="Trypanosomiasis" id="mwAu4">trypanosomiasis</a>. In addition, zebra hair is about as long as the mouthparts of these flies.<sup about="#mwt307" class="mw-ref reference" id="cite_ref-Caro_54-4" rel="dc:references" typeof="mw:Extension/ref" data-mw='{"name":"ref","attrs":{"name":"Caro"}}'><a href="#cite_note-Caro-54" id="mwAu8"><span class="mw-reflink-text" id="mwAvA"><span class="cite-bracket" id="mwAvE">[</span>54<span class="cite-bracket" id="mwAvI">]</span></span></a></sup> This hypothesis is the most strongly supported by the evidence.<sup about="#mwt308" class="mw-ref reference" id="cite_ref-Caro2_69-1" rel="dc:references" typeof="mw:Extension/ref" data-mw='{"name":"ref","attrs":{"name":"Caro2"}}'><a href="#cite_note-Caro2-69" id="mwAvM"><span class="mw-reflink-text" id="mwAvQ"><span class="cite-bracket" id="mwAvU">[</span>68<span class="cite-bracket" id="mwAvY">]</span></span></a></sup><sup about="#mwt309" class="mw-ref reference" id="cite_ref-Tombak2022_70-0" rel="dc:references" typeof="mw:Extension/ref" data-mw='{"name":"ref","attrs":{"name":"Tombak2022"}}'><a href="#cite_note-Tombak2022-70" id="mwAvc"><span class="mw-reflink-text" id="mwAvg"><span class="cite-bracket" id="mwAvk">[</span>69<span class="cite-bracket" id="mwAvo">]</span></span></a></sup> It was found that flies preferred landing on solidly coloured surfaces over those with black-and-white striped patterns in 1930 by biologist R. Harris,<sup about="#mwt301" class="mw-ref reference" id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTECaro20165_71-0" rel="dc:references" typeof="mw:Transclusion mw:Extension/ref" data-mw='{"name":"ref","attrs":{"group":"","name":"FOOTNOTECaro20165"},"body":{"id":"mw-reference-text-cite_note-FOOTNOTECaro20165-71"},"parts":[{"template":{"target":{"wt":"sfn","href":"./Template:Sfn"},"params":{"1":{"wt":"Caro"},"2":{"wt":"2016"},"p":{"wt":"5"}},"i":0}}]}'><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTECaro20165-71" id="mwAvs"><span class="mw-reflink-text" id="mwAvw"><span class="cite-bracket" id="mwAv0">[</span>70<span class="cite-bracket" id="mwAv4">]</span></span></a></sup> and this was proposed to have been a function of zebra stripes in a 1981 study.<sup about="#mwt312" class="mw-ref reference" id="cite_ref-72" rel="dc:references" typeof="mw:Extension/ref" data-mw='{"name":"ref","attrs":{},"body":{"id":"mw-reference-text-cite_note-72"}}'><a href="#cite_note-72" id="mwAv8"><span class="mw-reflink-text" id="mwAwA"><span class="cite-bracket" id="mwAwE">[</span>71<span class="cite-bracket" id="mwAwI">]</span></span></a></sup> A 2014 study found a correlation between striping and overlap with horse and <a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="//en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tsetse_fly" title="Tsetse fly" id="mwAwM">tsetse fly</a> populations and activity.<sup about="#mwt315" class="mw-ref reference" id="cite_ref-Caro_54-5" rel="dc:references" typeof="mw:Extension/ref" data-mw='{"name":"ref","attrs":{"name":"Caro"},"body":{"id":"mw-reference-text-cite_note-Caro-54"}}'><a href="#cite_note-Caro-54" id="mwAwQ"><span class="mw-reflink-text" id="mwAwU"><span class="cite-bracket" id="mwAwY">[</span>54<span class="cite-bracket" id="mwAwc">]</span></span></a></sup> Other studies have found that zebras are rarely targeted by these insect species.<sup about="#mwt304" class="mw-ref reference" id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTECaro2016196–197_73-0" rel="dc:references" typeof="mw:Transclusion mw:Extension/ref" data-mw='{"name":"ref","attrs":{"group":"","name":"FOOTNOTECaro2016196–197"},"body":{"id":"mw-reference-text-cite_note-FOOTNOTECaro2016196–197-73"},"parts":[{"template":{"target":{"wt":"sfn","href":"./Template:Sfn"},"params":{"1":{"wt":"Caro"},"2":{"wt":"2016"},"pp":{"wt":"196–197"}},"i":0}}]}'><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTECaro2016196–197-73" id="mwAwg"><span class="mw-reflink-text" id="mwAwk"><span class="cite-bracket" id="mwAwo">[</span>72<span class="cite-bracket" id="mwAws">]</span></span></a></sup> Caro and colleagues (2019) studied captive zebras and horses and observed that neither could deter flies from a distance, but zebra stripes kept flies from landing, both on zebras and horses dressed in zebra <a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="//en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Animal_print" title="Animal print" id="mwAww">print</a> coats.<sup about="#mwt318" class="mw-ref
