reference" id="cite_ref-Caro2_69-2" rel="dc:references" typeof="mw:Extension/ref" data-mw='{"name":"ref","attrs":{"name":"Caro2"},"body":{"id":"mw-reference-text-cite_note-Caro2-69"}}'><a href="#cite_note-Caro2-69" id="mwAw0"><span class="mw-reflink-text" id="mwAw4"><span class="cite-bracket" id="mwAw8">[</span>68<span class="cite-bracket" id="mwAxA">]</span></span></a></sup> There does not appear to be any difference in the effectiveness of repelling flies between the different zebra species; thus the difference in striping patterns may have evolved for other reasons.<sup about="#mwt321" class="mw-ref reference" id="cite_ref-Tombak2022_70-1" rel="dc:references" typeof="mw:Extension/ref" data-mw='{"name":"ref","attrs":{"name":"Tombak2022"},"body":{"id":"mw-reference-text-cite_note-Tombak2022-70"}}'><a href="#cite_note-Tombak2022-70" id="mwAxE"><span class="mw-reflink-text" id="mwAxI"><span class="cite-bracket" id="mwAxM">[</span>69<span class="cite-bracket" id="mwAxQ">]</span></span></a></sup> White or light stripes painted on dark bodies have also been found to reduce fly irritations in both cattle and humans.<sup about="#mwt324" class="mw-ref reference" id="cite_ref-74" rel="dc:references" typeof="mw:Extension/ref" data-mw='{"name":"ref","attrs":{},"body":{"id":"mw-reference-text-cite_note-74"}}'><a href="#cite_note-74" id="mwAxU"><span class="mw-reflink-text" id="mwAxY"><span class="cite-bracket" id="mwAxc">[</span>73<span class="cite-bracket" id="mwAxg">]</span></span></a></sup><sup about="#mwt327" class="mw-ref reference" id="cite_ref-75" rel="dc:references" typeof="mw:Extension/ref" data-mw='{"name":"ref","attrs":{},"body":{"id":"mw-reference-text-cite_note-75"}}'><a href="#cite_note-75" id="mwAxk"><span class="mw-reflink-text" id="mwAxo"><span class="cite-bracket" id="mwAxs">[</span>74<span class="cite-bracket" id="mwAxw">]</span></span></a></sup> How the stripes repel flies is less clear.<sup about="#mwt328" class="mw-ref reference" id="cite_ref-Tombak2022_70-2" rel="dc:references" typeof="mw:Extension/ref" data-mw='{"name":"ref","attrs":{"name":"Tombak2022"}}'><a href="#cite_note-Tombak2022-70" id="mwAx0"><span class="mw-reflink-text" id="mwAx4"><span class="cite-bracket" id="mwAx8">[</span>69<span class="cite-bracket" id="mwAyA">]</span></span></a></sup> A 2012 study concluded that they disrupt the <a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="//en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Polarization_(physics)" title="Polarization (physics)" class="mw-redirect" id="mwAyE">polarised</a> light patterns these insects use to locate water and habitat,<sup about="#mwt331" class="mw-ref reference" id="cite_ref-76" rel="dc:references" typeof="mw:Extension/ref" data-mw='{"name":"ref","attrs":{},"body":{"id":"mw-reference-text-cite_note-76"}}'><a href="#cite_note-76" id="mwAyI"><span class="mw-reflink-text" id="mwAyM"><span class="cite-bracket" id="mwAyQ">[</span>75<span class="cite-bracket" id="mwAyU">]</span></span></a></sup> though subsequent studies have refuted this.<sup about="#mwt332" class="mw-ref reference" id="cite_ref-Caro2023_77-0" rel="dc:references" typeof="mw:Extension/ref" data-mw='{"name":"ref","attrs":{"name":"Caro2023"}}'><a href="#cite_note-Caro2023-77" id="mwAyY"><span class="mw-reflink-text" id="mwAyc"><span class="cite-bracket" id="mwAyg">[</span>76<span class="cite-bracket" id="mwAyk">]</span></span></a></sup><sup about="#mwt333" class="mw-ref reference" id="cite_ref-Takács2022_78-0" rel="dc:references" typeof="mw:Extension/ref" data-mw='{"name":"ref","attrs":{"name":"Takács2022"}}'><a href="#cite_note-Takács2022-78" id="mwAyo"><span class="mw-reflink-text" id="mwAys"><span class="cite-bracket" id="mwAyw">[</span>77<span class="cite-bracket" id="mwAy0">]</span></span></a></sup><sup about="#mwt336" class="mw-ref reference" id="cite_ref-79" rel="dc:references" typeof="mw:Extension/ref" data-mw='{"name":"ref","attrs":{},"body":{"id":"mw-reference-text-cite_note-79"}}'><a href="#cite_note-79" id="mwAy4"><span class="mw-reflink-text" id="mwAy8"><span class="cite-bracket" id="mwAzA">[</span>78<span class="cite-bracket" id="mwAzE">]</span></span></a></sup> Stripes do not appear to work like a barber pole against flies since <a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="//en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Check_(pattern)" title="Check (pattern)" id="mwAzI">checkered patterns</a> also repel them.<sup about="#mwt337" class="mw-ref reference" id="cite_ref-Caro2023_77-1" rel="dc:references" typeof="mw:Extension/ref" data-mw='{"name":"ref","attrs":{"name":"Caro2023"}}'><a href="#cite_note-Caro2023-77" id="mwAzM"><span class="mw-reflink-text" id="mwAzQ"><span class="cite-bracket" id="mwAzU">[</span>76<span class="cite-bracket" id="mwAzY">]</span></span></a></sup><sup about="#mwt340" class="mw-ref reference" id="cite_ref-80" rel="dc:references" typeof="mw:Extension/ref" data-mw='{"name":"ref","attrs":{},"body":{"id":"mw-reference-text-cite_note-80"}}'><a href="#cite_note-80" id="mwAzc"><span class="mw-reflink-text" id="mwAzg"><span class="cite-bracket" id="mwAzk">[</span>79<span class="cite-bracket" id="mwAzo">]</span></span></a></sup> There is also little evidence that zebra stripes confuse the insects via visual distortion or <a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="//en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Aliasing" title="Aliasing" id="mwAzs">aliasing</a>.<sup about="#mwt341" class="mw-ref reference" id="cite_ref-Caro2023_77-2" rel="dc:references" typeof="mw:Extension/ref" data-mw='{"name":"ref","attrs":{"name":"Caro2023"}}'><a href="#cite_note-Caro2023-77" id="mwAzw"><span class="mw-reflink-text" id="mwAz0"><span class="cite-bracket" id="mwAz4">[</span>76<span class="cite-bracket" id="mwAz8">]</span></span></a></sup> Takács and colleagues (2022) suggest that, when the animal is in sunlight, temperature gradients between the warmer dark stripes and cooler white stripes prevent horseflies from detecting the warm blood vessels underneath.<sup about="#mwt344" class="mw-ref reference" id="cite_ref-Takács2022_78-1" rel="dc:references" typeof="mw:Extension/ref" data-mw='{"name":"ref","attrs":{"name":"Takács2022"},"body":{"id":"mw-reference-text-cite_note-Takács2022-78"}}'><a href="#cite_note-Takács2022-78" id="mwA0A"><span class="mw-reflink-text" id="mwA0E"><span class="cite-bracket" id="mwA0I">[</span>77<span class="cite-bracket" id="mwA0M">]</span></span></a></sup> Caro and colleagues (2023) conclude that the high colour contrast and relative thinness of the patterns make it difficult for the insects to find a place to land.<sup about="#mwt347" class="mw-ref reference" id="cite_ref-Caro2023_77-3" rel="dc:references" typeof="mw:Extension/ref" data-mw='{"name":"ref","attrs":{"name":"Caro2023"},"body":{"id":"mw-reference-text-cite_note-Caro2023-77"}}'><a href="#cite_note-Caro2023-77" id="mwA0Q"><span class="mw-reflink-text" id="mwA0U"><span class="cite-bracket" id="mwA0Y">[</span>76<span class="cite-bracket" id="mwA0c">]</span></span></a></sup></li></ul></section></section></section><section data-mw-section-id="9" id="mwA0g" aria-labelledby="Behaviour_and_ecology"><div class="mw-heading mw-heading2"><h2 id="Behaviour_and_ecology">Behaviour and ecology</h2></div><figure class="mw-default-size mw-halign-right" typeof="mw:File/Thumb" id="mwA0k"><a href="//en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Dust_bathing_mountain_zebra,_Namibia.jpg" class="mw-file-description" id="mwA0o"><img alt="Mountain zebra dust bathing" resource="//en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Dust_bathing_mountain_zebra,_Namibia.jpg" src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/7/7a/Dust_bathing_mountain_zebra%2C_Namibia.jpg/250px-Dust_bathing_mountain_zebra%2C_Namibia.jpg" decoding="async" data-file-width="4928" data-file-height="3264" data-file-type="bitmap" height="166" width="250" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/7/7a/Dust_bathing_mountain_zebra%2C_Namibia.jpg/500px-Dust_bathing_mountain_zebra%2C_Namibia.jpg 2x" class="mw-file-element" id="mwA0s"/></a><figcaption id="mwA0w">Mountain zebra dustbathing in <a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="//en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Namibia" title="Namibia" id="mwA00">Namibia</a></figcaption></figure><p id="mwA04">Zebras may travel or <a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="//en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Animal_migration" title="Animal migration" id="mwA08">migrate</a> to wetter areas during the dry season.<sup about="#mwt351" class="mw-ref reference" id="cite_ref-Grub_1981_28-2" rel="dc:references" typeof="mw:Extension/ref" data-mw='{"name":"ref","attrs":{"name":"Grub 1981"}}'><a href="#cite_note-Grub_1981-28" id="mwA1A"><span class="mw-reflink-text" id="mwA1E"><span class="cite-bracket" id="mwA1I">[</span>28<span class="cite-bracket" id="mwA1M">]</span></span></a></sup><sup about="#mwt352" class="mw-ref reference" id="cite_ref-Estes_1991_29-2" rel="dc:references" typeof="mw:Extension/ref" data-mw='{"name":"ref","attrs":{"name":"Estes 1991"}}'><a href="#cite_note-Estes_1991-29" id="mwA1Q"><span class="mw-reflink-text" id="mwA1U"><span class="cite-bracket" id="mwA1Y">[</span>29<span class="cite-bracket" id="mwA1c">]</span></span></a></sup> Plains zebras have been recorded travelling <span about="#mwt348" typeof="mw:Transclusion" id="mwA1g" data-mw='{"parts":[{"template":{"target":{"wt":"cvt","href":"./Template:Cvt"},"params":{"1":{"wt":"500"},"2":{"wt":"km"},"3":{"wt":"mi"}},"i":0}}]}'>500</span><span typeof="mw:Entity" about="#mwt348"> </span><span about="#mwt348">km (310</span><span typeof="mw:Entity" about="#mwt348"> </span><span about="#mwt348">mi)</span> between Namibia and Botswana, the longest land migration of mammals in Africa.<sup about="#mwt355" class="mw-ref reference" id="cite_ref-81" rel="dc:references" typeof="mw:Extension/ref" data-mw='{"name":"ref","attrs":{},"body":{"id":"mw-reference-text-cite_note-81"}}'><a href="#cite_note-81" id="mwA1k"><span class="mw-reflink-text" id="mwA1o"><span class="cite-bracket" id="mwA1s">[</span>80<span class="cite-bracket" id="mwA1w">]</span></span></a></sup> When migrating, they appear to rely on some memory of the locations where foraging conditions were best and may predict conditions months after their arrival.<sup about="#mwt358" class="mw-ref reference" id="cite_ref-82" rel="dc:references" typeof="mw:Extension/ref" data-mw='{"name":"ref","attrs":{},"body":{"id":"mw-reference-text-cite_note-82"}}'><a href="#cite_note-82" id="mwA10"><span class="mw-reflink-text" id="mwA14"><span class="cite-bracket" id="mwA18">[</span>81<span class="cite-bracket" id="mwA2A">]</span></span></a></sup> Plains zebras are more water-dependent and live in <a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="//en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mesic_habitat" title="Mesic habitat" id="mwA2E">moister</a> environments than other species. They usually can be found <span about="#mwt349" typeof="mw:Transclusion" id="mwA2I" data-mw='{"parts":[{"template":{"target":{"wt":"cvt","href":"./Template:Cvt"},"params":{"1":{"wt":"10"},"2":{"wt":"–"},"3":{"wt":"12"},"4":{"wt":"km"}},"i":0}}]}'>10–12</span><span typeof="mw:Entity" about="#mwt349"> </span><span about="#mwt349">km (6.2–7.5</span><span typeof="mw:Entity" about="#mwt349"> </span><span about="#mwt349">mi)</span> from a water source.<sup about="#mwt359" class="mw-ref reference" id="cite_ref-Grub_1981_28-3" rel="dc:references" typeof="mw:Extension/ref" data-mw='{"name":"ref","attrs":{"name":"Grub 1981"}}'><a href="#cite_note-Grub_1981-28" id="mwA2M"><span class="mw-reflink-text" id="mwA2Q"><span class="cite-bracket" id="mwA2U">[</span>28<span class="cite-bracket" id="mwA2Y">]</span></span></a></sup><sup about="#mwt360" class="mw-ref reference" id="cite_ref-Estes_1991_29-3" rel="dc:references" typeof="mw:Extension/ref" data-mw='{"name":"ref","attrs":{"name":"Estes 1991"}}'><a href="#cite_note-Estes_1991-29" id="mwA2c"><span class="mw-reflink-text" id="mwA2g"><span class="cite-bracket" id="mwA2k">[</span>29<span class="cite-bracket" id="mwA2o">]</span></span></a></sup><sup about="#mwt361" class="mw-ref reference" id="cite_ref-Skinner_31-2" rel="dc:references" typeof="mw:Extension/ref" data-mw='{"name":"ref","attrs":{"name":"Skinner"}}'><a href="#cite_note-Skinner-31" id="mwA2s"><span class="mw-reflink-text" id="mwA2w"><span class="cite-bracket" id="mwA20">[</span>31<span class="cite-bracket" id="mwA24">]</span></span></a></sup> Grévy's zebras can survive almost a week without water but will drink it every day when given the chance, and their bodies maintain water better than cattle.<sup about="#mwt364" class="mw-ref reference" id="cite_ref-83" rel="dc:references" typeof="mw:Extension/ref" data-mw='{"name":"ref","attrs":{},"body":{"id":"mw-reference-text-cite_note-83"}}'><a href="#cite_note-83" id="mwA28"><span class="mw-reflink-text" id="mwA3A"><span class="cite-bracket" id="mwA3E">[</span>82<span class="cite-bracket" id="mwA3I">]</span></span></a></sup><sup about="#mwt365" class="mw-ref reference" id="cite_ref-Churcher_1993_25-4" rel="dc:references" typeof="mw:Extension/ref" data-mw='{"name":"ref","attrs":{"name":"Churcher 1993"}}'><a href="#cite_note-Churcher_1993-25" id="mwA3M"><span class="mw-reflink-text" id="mwA3Q"><span class="cite-bracket" id="mwA3U">[</span>25<span class="cite-bracket" id="mwA3Y">]</span></span></a></sup> Mountain zebras can be found at elevations of up to <span about="#mwt350" typeof="mw:Transclusion" id="mwA3c" data-mw='{"parts":[{"template":{"target":{"wt":"cvt","href":"./Template:Cvt"},"params":{"1":{"wt":"2000"},"2":{"wt":"m"}},"i":0}}]}'>2,000</span><span typeof="mw:Entity" about="#mwt350"> </span><span about="#mwt350">m (6,600</span><span typeof="mw:Entity" about="#mwt350"> </span><span about="#mwt350">ft)</span>.<sup about="#mwt368" class="mw-ref reference" id="cite_ref-84" rel="dc:references" typeof="mw:Extension/ref" data-mw='{"name":"ref","attrs":{},"body":{"id":"mw-reference-text-cite_note-84"}}'><a href="#cite_note-84" id="mwA3g"><span class="mw-reflink-text" id="mwA3k"><span class="cite-bracket" id="mwA3o">[</span>83<span class="cite-bracket" id="mwA3s">]</span></span></a></sup> Zebras sleep up to seven hours a day, standing up during the day and lying down during the night. They regularly use various objects as rubbing posts and will <a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="//en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dust_bathing" title="Dust bathing" id="mwA3w">roll on the ground</a>.<sup about="#mwt369" class="mw-ref reference" id="cite_ref-Estes_1991_29-4" rel="dc:references" typeof="mw:Extension/ref" data-mw='{"name":"ref","attrs":{"name":"Estes 1991"}}'><a href="#cite_note-Estes_1991-29" id="mwA30"><span class="mw-reflink-text" id="mwA34"><span class="cite-bracket" id="mwA38">[</span>29<span class="cite-bracket" id="mwA4A">]</span></span></a></sup></p><figure class="mw-default-size mw-halign-left" typeof="mw:File/Thumb" id="mwA4E"><a href="//en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Cebras_de_Burchell_(Equus_quagga_burchellii),_vista_aérea_del_delta_del_Okavango,_Botsuana,_2018-08-01,_DD_30.jpg" class="mw-file-description" id="mwA4I"><img alt="Plains zebras drinking at a river" resource="//en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Cebras_de_Burchell_(Equus_quagga_burchellii),_vista_aérea_del_delta_del_Okavango,_Botsuana,_2018-08-01,_DD_30.jpg" src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/5/5b/Cebras_de_Burchell_%28Equus_quagga_burchellii%29%2C_vista_a%C3%A9rea_del_delta_del_Okavango%2C_Botsuana%2C_2018-08-01%2C_DD_30.jpg/250px-Cebras_de_Burchell_%28Equus_quagga_burchellii%29%2C_vista_a%C3%A9rea_del_delta_del_Okavango%2C_Botsuana%2C_2018-08-01%2C_DD_30.jpg" decoding="async" data-file-width="5067" data-file-height="3274" data-file-type="bitmap" height="162" width="250" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/5/5b/Cebras_de_Burchell_%28Equus_quagga_burchellii%29%2C_vista_a%C3%A9rea_del_delta_del_Okavango%2C_Botsuana%2C_2018-08-01%2C_DD_30.jpg/500px-Cebras_de_Burchell_%28Equus_quagga_burchellii%29%2C_vista_a%C3%A9rea_del_delta_del_Okavango%2C_Botsuana%2C_2018-08-01%2C_DD_30.jpg 2x" class="mw-file-element" id="mwA4M"/></a><figcaption id="mwA4Q">Plains zebras at <a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="//en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Okavango_Delta" title="Okavango Delta" id="mwA4U">Okavango Delta</a>, <a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="//en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Botswana" title="Botswana" id="mwA4Y">Botswana</a></figcaption></figure><p id="mwA4c">A zebra's diet is mostly <a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="//en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Grass" title="Grass" id="mwA4g">grasses</a> and <a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="//en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sedge" title="Sedge" class="mw-redirect" id="mwA4k">sedges</a>, but they will opportunistically consume <a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="//en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bark_(botany)" title="Bark (botany)" id="mwA4o">bark</a>, leaves, buds, fruits, and roots. Compared to <a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="//en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ruminantia" title="Ruminantia" class="mw-redirect" id="mwA4s">ruminants</a>, zebras have a simpler and less efficient digestive system. Nevertheless, they can subsist on lower-quality vegetation. Zebras may spend 60–80% of their time feeding, depending on the availability of vegetation.<sup about="#mwt370" class="mw-ref reference" id="cite_ref-MacDonald_9-6" rel="dc:references" typeof="mw:Extension/ref" data-mw='{"name":"ref","attrs":{"name":"MacDonald"}}'><a href="#cite_note-MacDonald-9" id="mwA4w"><span class="mw-reflink-text" id="mwA40"><span class="cite-bracket" id="mwA44">[</span>9<span class="cite-bracket" id="mwA48">]</span></span></a></sup><sup about="#mwt371" class="mw-ref reference" id="cite_ref-Estes_1991_29-5" rel="dc:references" typeof="mw:Extension/ref" data-mw='{"name":"ref","attrs":{"name":"Estes 1991"}}'><a href="#cite_note-Estes_1991-29" id="mwA5A"><span class="mw-reflink-text" id="mwA5E"><span class="cite-bracket" id="mwA5I">[</span>29<span class="cite-bracket" id="mwA5M">]</span></span></a></sup> The plains zebra is a pioneer grazer, mowing down the upper, less nutritious grass canopy and preparing the way for more specialised grazers like <a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="//en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wildebeest" title="Wildebeest" id="mwA5Q">wildebeest</a>, which depend on shorter and more nutritious grasses below.<sup about="#mwt374" class="mw-ref reference" id="cite_ref-85" rel="dc:references" typeof="mw:Extension/ref" data-mw='{"name":"ref","attrs":{},"body":{"id":"mw-reference-text-cite_note-85"}}'><a href="#cite_note-85" id="mwA5U"><span class="mw-reflink-text" id="mwA5Y"><span class="cite-bracket" id="mwA5c">[</span>84<span class="cite-bracket" id="mwA5g">]</span></span></a></sup></p><p id="mwA5k">Zebras are preyed on mainly by lions. <a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="//en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Leopards" title="Leopards" class="mw-redirect" id="mwA5o">Leopards</a>, <a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="//en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cheetahs" title="Cheetahs" class="mw-redirect" id="mwA5s">cheetahs</a>, <a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="//en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Spotted_hyenas" title="Spotted hyenas" class="mw-redirect" id="mwA5w">spotted hyenas</a>, <a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="//en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Brown_hyena" title="Brown hyena" id="mwA50">brown hyenas</a> and <a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="//en.wikipedia.org/wiki/African_wild_dog" title="African wild dog" id="mwA54">wild dogs</a> pose less of a threat to adults.<sup about="#mwt375" class="mw-ref reference" id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTECaro201661–63_86-0" rel="dc:references" typeof="mw:Transclusion mw:Extension/ref" data-mw='{"name":"ref","attrs":{"group":"","name":"FOOTNOTECaro201661–63"},"body":{"id":"mw-reference-text-cite_note-FOOTNOTECaro201661–63-86"},"parts":[{"template":{"target":{"wt":"sfn","href":"./Template:Sfn"},"params":{"1":{"wt":"Caro"},"2":{"wt":"2016"},"pp":{"wt":"61–63"}},"i":0}}]}'><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTECaro201661–63-86" id="mwA58"><span class="mw-reflink-text" id="mwA6A"><span class="cite-bracket" id="mwA6E">[</span>85<span class="cite-bracket" id="mwA6I">]</span></span></a></sup> Biting and kicking are a zebra's defense tactics. When threatened by lions, zebras flee, and when caught they are rarely effective in fighting off the big cats.<sup about="#mwt378" class="mw-ref reference" id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTECaro201661–62_87-0" rel="dc:references" typeof="mw:Transclusion mw:Extension/ref" data-mw='{"name":"ref","attrs":{"group":"","name":"FOOTNOTECaro201661–62"},"body":{"id":"mw-reference-text-cite_note-FOOTNOTECaro201661–62-87"},"parts":[{"template":{"target":{"wt":"sfn","href":"./Template:Sfn"},"params":{"1":{"wt":"Caro"},"2":{"wt":"2016"},"p":{"wt":"61–62"}},"i":0}}]}'><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTECaro201661–62-87" id="mwA6M"><span class="mw-reflink-text" id="mwA6Q"><span class="cite-bracket" id="mwA6U">[</span>86<span class="cite-bracket" id="mwA6Y">]</span></span></a></sup> In one study, the maximum speed of a zebra was found to be <span about="#mwt381" typeof="mw:Transclusion" id="mwA6c" data-mw='{"parts":[{"template":{"target":{"wt":"cvt","href":"./Template:Cvt"},"params":{"1":{"wt":"50"},"2":{"wt":"km/h"}},"i":0}}]}'>50</span><span typeof="mw:Entity" about="#mwt381"> </span><span about="#mwt381">km/h (31</span><span typeof="mw:Entity" about="#mwt381"> </span><span about="#mwt381">mph)</span> while a lion was measured at <span about="#mwt382" typeof="mw:Transclusion" id="mwA6g" data-mw='{"parts":[{"template":{"target":{"wt":"cvt","href":"./Template:Cvt"},"params":{"1":{"wt":"74"},"2":{"wt":"km/h"}},"i":0}}]}'>74</span><span typeof="mw:Entity" about="#mwt382"> </span><span about="#mwt382">km/h (46</span><span typeof="mw:Entity" about="#mwt382"> </span><span about="#mwt382">mph)</span>. Zebras do not escape lions by speed alone but by sideways turning, especially when the cat is close behind.<sup about="#mwt388" class="mw-ref reference" id="cite_ref-88" rel="dc:references" typeof="mw:Extension/ref" data-mw='{"name":"ref","attrs":{},"body":{"id":"mw-reference-text-cite_note-88"}}'><a href="#cite_note-88" id="mwA6k"><span class="mw-reflink-text" id="mwA6o"><span class="cite-bracket" id="mwA6s">[</span>87<span class="cite-bracket" id="mwA6w">]</span></span></a></sup> With smaller predators like hyenas and dogs, zebras may act more aggressively, especially in defense of their young.<sup about="#mwt383" class="mw-ref reference" id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTECaro201662–63_89-0" rel="dc:references" typeof="mw:Transclusion mw:Extension/ref" data-mw='{"name":"ref","attrs":{"group":"","name":"FOOTNOTECaro201662–63"},"body":{"id":"mw-reference-text-cite_note-FOOTNOTECaro201662–63-89"},"parts":[{"template":{"target":{"wt":"sfn","href":"./Template:Sfn"},"params":{"1":{"wt":"Caro"},"2":{"wt":"2016"},"pp":{"wt":"62–63"}},"i":0}}]}'><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTECaro201662–63-89" id="mwA60"><span class="mw-reflink-text" id="mwA64"><span class="cite-bracket" id="mwA68">[</span>88<span class="cite-bracket" id="mwA7A">]</span></span></a></sup></p><section data-mw-section-id="10" id="mwA7E" aria-labelledby="Social_behaviour"><div class="mw-heading mw-heading3"><h3 id="Social_behaviour">Social behaviour</h3></div><span class="mw-empty-elt" about="#mwt389" typeof="mw:Transclusion" id="mwA7I" data-mw='{"parts":[{"template":{"target":{"wt":"See also","href":"./Template:See_also"},"params":{"1":{"wt":"Horse behaviour"}},"i":0}}]}'><link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1353705441" about="#mwt390" typeof="mw:Extension/templatestyles" data-mw='{"name":"templatestyles","attrs":{"src":"Module:Hatnote/styles.css"},"body":{"extsrc":""}}'/></span><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable" about="#mwt389" id="mwA7M">See also: <a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="//en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Horse_behaviour" title="Horse behaviour" class="mw-redirect">Horse behaviour</a></div><figure class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Thumb" id="mwA7Q"><a href="//en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Zebra_Botswana_edit02.jpg" class="mw-file-description" id="mwA7U"><img alt="A group of six plains zebra" resource="//en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Zebra_Botswana_edit02.jpg" src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/8/83/Zebra_Botswana_edit02.jpg/250px-Zebra_Botswana_edit02.jpg" decoding="async" data-file-width="1234" data-file-height="1168" data-file-type="bitmap" height="180" width="190" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/8/83/Zebra_Botswana_edit02.jpg/500px-Zebra_Botswana_edit02.jpg 2x" class="mw-file-element mw-file-upright" style="--mw-file-upright: 0.75" id="mwA7Y"/></a><figcaption id="mwA7c">A plains zebra group </figcaption></figure><p id="mwA7g">Zebra species have two basic social structures. Plains and mountain zebras live in stable, closed family groups or <a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="//en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Harem_(zoology)" title="Harem (zoology)" id="mwA7k">harems</a> consisting of one <a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="//en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Stallion" title="Stallion" id="mwA7o">stallion</a>, several <a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="//en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mares" title="Mares" class="mw-redirect" id="mwA7s">mares</a>, and their offspring. These groups have their own <a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="//en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Home_ranges" title="Home ranges" class="mw-redirect" id="mwA7w">home ranges</a>, which overlap, and they tend to be nomadic. Stallions form and expand their harems by herding young mares away from their birth harems. The stability of the group remains even when the family stallion is displaced. Plains zebras groups gather into large herds and may create temporarily stable subgroups within a herd, allowing individuals to interact with those outside their group. Females in harems can spend more time feeding, and gain protection both for them and their young. They have a linear <a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="//en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dominance_(ethology)" title="Dominance (ethology)" class="mw-redirect" id="mwA70">dominance hierarchy</a> with the high-ranking females having lived in the group longest. While traveling, the most dominant females and their offspring lead the group, followed by the next most dominant; the family stallion trails behind. Young of both sexes leave their natal groups as they mature; females are usually herded by outside males to become part of their harems.<sup about="#mwt391" class="mw-ref reference" id="cite_ref-MacDonald_9-7" rel="dc:references" typeof="mw:Extension/ref" data-mw='{"name":"ref","attrs":{"name":"MacDonald"}}'><a href="#cite_note-MacDonald-9" id="mwA74"><span class="mw-reflink-text" id="mwA78"><span class="cite-bracket" id="mwA8A">[</span>9<span class="cite-bracket" id="mwA8E">]</span></span></a></sup><sup about="#mwt392" class="mw-ref reference" id="cite_ref-Estes_1991_29-6" rel="dc:references" typeof="mw:Extension/ref" data-mw='{"name":"ref","attrs":{"name":"Estes 1991"}}'><a href="#cite_note-Estes_1991-29" id="mwA8I"><span class="mw-reflink-text" id="mwA8M"><span class="cite-bracket" id="mwA8Q">[</span>29<span class="cite-bracket" id="mwA8U">]</span></span></a></sup><sup about="#mwt393" class="mw-ref reference" id="cite_ref-Rubenstein_1986_90-0" rel="dc:references" typeof="mw:Extension/ref" data-mw='{"name":"ref","attrs":{"name":"Rubenstein 1986"}}'><a href="#cite_note-Rubenstein_1986-90" id="mwA8Y"><span class="mw-reflink-text" id="mwA8c"><span class="cite-bracket" id="mwA8g">[</span>89<span class="cite-bracket" id="mwA8k">]</span></span></a></sup></p><p id="mwA8o">In the more arid-living Grévy's zebras, adults have more fluid associations and adult males establish large <a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="//en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Territory_(animal)" title="Territory (animal)" id="mwA8s">territories</a>, marked by dung piles, and mate with the females that enter them.<sup about="#mwt394" class="mw-ref reference" id="cite_ref-Estes_1991_29-7" rel="dc:references" typeof="mw:Extension/ref" data-mw='{"name":"ref","attrs":{"name":"Estes 1991"}}'><a href="#cite_note-Estes_1991-29" id="mwA8w"><span class="mw-reflink-text" id="mwA80"><span class="cite-bracket" id="mwA84">[</span>29<span class="cite-bracket" id="mwA88">]</span></span></a></sup><sup about="#mwt395" class="mw-ref reference" id="cite_ref-MacDonald_9-8" rel="dc:references" typeof="mw:Extension/ref" data-mw='{"name":"ref","attrs":{"name":"MacDonald"}}'><a href="#cite_note-MacDonald-9" id="mwA9A"><span class="mw-reflink-text" id="mwA9E"><span class="cite-bracket" id="mwA9I">[</span>9<span class="cite-bracket" id="mwA9M">]</span></span></a></sup> Grazing and drinking areas tend to be separated in these environments and the most dominant males establish territories near watering holes, which attract females with dependent foals and those who simply want a drink, while less dominant males control territories away from water with more vegetation, and only attract mares without foals.<sup about="#mwt396" class="mw-ref reference" id="cite_ref-Rubenstein_2010_91-0" rel="dc:references" typeof="mw:Extension/ref" data-mw='{"name":"ref","attrs":{"name":"Rubenstein 2010"}}'><a href="#cite_note-Rubenstein_2010-91" id="mwA9Q"><span class="mw-reflink-text" id="mwA9U"><span class="cite-bracket" id="mwA9Y">[</span>90<span class="cite-bracket" id="mwA9c">]</span></span></a></sup> Mares may travel through several territories but remain in one when they have young. Staying in a territory offers a female protection from harassment by outside males, as well as access to resources.<sup about="#mwt399" class="mw-ref reference" id="cite_ref-Rubenstein_1986_90-1" rel="dc:references" typeof="mw:Extension/ref" data-mw='{"name":"ref","attrs":{"name":"Rubenstein 1986"},"body":{"id":"mw-reference-text-cite_note-Rubenstein_1986-90"}}'><a href="#cite_note-Rubenstein_1986-90" id="mwA9g"><span class="mw-reflink-text" id="mwA9k"><span class="cite-bracket" id="mwA9o">[</span>89<span class="cite-bracket" id="mwA9s">]</span></span></a></sup><sup about="#mwt402" class="mw-ref reference" id="cite_ref-92" rel="dc:references" typeof="mw:Extension/ref" data-mw='{"name":"ref","attrs":{},"body":{"id":"mw-reference-text-cite_note-92"}}'><a href="#cite_note-92" id="mwA9w"><span class="mw-reflink-text" id="mwA90"><span class="cite-bracket" id="mwA94">[</span>91<span class="cite-bracket" id="mwA98">]</span></span></a></sup></p><figure class="mw-default-size mw-halign-left" typeof="mw:File/Thumb" id="mwA-A"><a href="//en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Grevy's_zebra_group.jpg" class="mw-file-description" id="mwA-E"><img alt="Three Grévy's zebras grazing" resource="//en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Grevy's_zebra_group.jpg" src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/0/0a/Grevy%27s_zebra_group.jpg/250px-Grevy%27s_zebra_group.jpg" decoding="async" data-file-width="2809" data-file-height="2000" data-file-type="bitmap" height="178" width="250" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/0/0a/Grevy%27s_zebra_group.jpg/500px-Grevy%27s_zebra_group.jpg 2x" class="mw-file-element" id="mwA-I"/></a><figcaption id="mwA-M">Group of Grévy's zebras grazing</figcaption></figure><p id="mwA-Q">In all species, excess males gather in <a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="//en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bachelor_herd" title="Bachelor herd" id="mwA-U">bachelor groups</a>. These are typically young males that are not yet ready to establish a harem or territory.<sup about="#mwt403" class="mw-ref reference" id="cite_ref-MacDonald_9-9" rel="dc:references" typeof="mw:Extension/ref" data-mw='{"name":"ref","attrs":{"name":"MacDonald"}}'><a href="#cite_note-MacDonald-9" id="mwA-Y"><span class="mw-reflink-text" id="mwA-c"><span class="cite-bracket" id="mwA-g">[</span>9<span class="cite-bracket" id="mwA-k">]</span></span></a></sup><sup about="#mwt404" class="mw-ref reference" id="cite_ref-Estes_1991_29-8" rel="dc:references" typeof="mw:Extension/ref" data-mw='{"name":"ref","attrs":{"name":"Estes 1991"}}'><a href="#cite_note-Estes_1991-29" id="mwA-o"><span class="mw-reflink-text" id="mwA-s"><span class="cite-bracket" id="mwA-w">[</span>29<span class="cite-bracket" id="mwA-0">]</span></span></a></sup> With the plains zebra, the oldest males are the most dominant and group membership is stable.<sup about="#mwt405" class="mw-ref reference" id="cite_ref-Estes_1991_29-9" rel="dc:references" typeof="mw:Extension/ref" data-mw='{"name":"ref","attrs":{"name":"Estes 1991"}}'><a href="#cite_note-Estes_1991-29" id="mwA-4"><span class="mw-reflink-text" id="mwA-8"><span class="cite-bracket" id="mwA_A">[</span>29<span class="cite-bracket" id="mwA_E">]</span></span></a></sup> Bachelor groups tend to be at the boundaries of herds and during group movements, the bachelors follow behind or along the sides.<sup about="#mwt408" class="mw-ref reference" id="cite_ref-Skinner_31-3" rel="dc:references" typeof="mw:Extension/ref" data-mw='{"name":"ref","attrs":{"name":"Skinner"},"body":{"id":"mw-reference-text-cite_note-Skinner-31"}}'><a href="#cite_note-Skinner-31" id="mwA_I"><span class="mw-reflink-text" id="mwA_M"><span class="cite-bracket" id="mwA_Q">[</span>31<span class="cite-bracket" id="mwA_U">]</span></span></a></sup> Mountain zebra bachelor groups may also include young females that have left their natal group early, as well as old, former harem males. A territorial Grévy's zebra stallion may allow non-territorial bachelors in their territory, however when a mare in <a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="//en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Oestrous" title="Oestrous" class="mw-redirect" id="mwA_Y">oestrous</a> is present the territorial stallion keeps other stallions at bay. Bachelors prepare for their future harem roles with play fights and greeting/challenge rituals, which make up most of their activities.<sup about="#mwt409" class="mw-ref reference" id="cite_ref-Estes_1991_29-10" rel="dc:references" typeof="mw:Extension/ref" data-mw='{"name":"ref","attrs":{"name":"Estes 1991"}}'><a href="#cite_note-Estes_1991-29" id="mwA_c"><span class="mw-reflink-text" id="mwA_g"><span class="cite-bracket" id="mwA_k">[</span>29<span class="cite-bracket" id="mwA_o">]</span></span></a></sup></p><p id="mwA_s">Fights between males usually occur over mates and involve biting and kicking. In plains zebra, stallions fight each other over recently matured mares to bring into their group and her father will fight off all suitors trying to abduct her. As long as a harem stallion is healthy, he is not usually challenged. Only unhealthy stallions have their harems taken over, and even then, the new stallion slowly takes over, peacefully displacing the old one. <a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="//en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Agonistic_behaviour" title="Agonistic behaviour" id="mwA_w">Agonistic behaviour</a> between male Grévy's zebras occurs at the border of their territories.<sup about="#mwt410" class="mw-ref reference" id="cite_ref-Estes_1991_29-11" rel="dc:references" typeof="mw:Extension/ref" data-mw='{"name":"ref","attrs":{"name":"Estes 1991"}}'><a href="#cite_note-Estes_1991-29" id="mwA_0"><span class="mw-reflink-text" id="mwA_4"><span class="cite-bracket" id="mwA_8">[</span>29<span class="cite-bracket" id="mwBAA">]</span></span></a></sup></p></section><section data-mw-section-id="11" id="mwBAE" aria-labelledby="Communication"><div class="mw-heading mw-heading3"><h3 id="Communication">Communication</h3></div><span class="mw-empty-elt" about="#mwt411" typeof="mw:Transclusion" id="mwBAI" data-mw='{"parts":[{"template":{"target":{"wt":"Listen\n","href":"./Template:Listen"},"params":{"filename":{"wt":"Grévys zebra (Sound Effects).ogg"},"title":{"wt":"Grévy&apos;s zebra call"},"description":{"wt":"The call of a Grevy&apos;s zebra at Lincoln Park Zoo, Chicago, Illinois."}},"i":0}}]}'><style data-mw-deduplicate="TemplateStyles:r1314755338" typeof="mw:Extension/templatestyles" about="#mwt412" data-mw='{"name":"templatestyles","attrs":{"src":"Module:Side box/styles.css"},"body":{"extsrc":""}}'>.mw-parser-output .side-box{margin:4px .infobox .side-box{font-size:100%}.mw-parser-output .side-box-abovebelow,.mw-parser-output .side-box-text{padding:0.25em 0.9em}.mw-parser-output .side-box-image{padding:2px 0 2px
