.listen-center{float:none;margin-left:auto;margin-right:auto}}</style></span><div class="side-box side-box-right listen noprint" about="#mwt411"><span class="mw-empty-elt"><style data-mw-deduplicate="TemplateStyles:r1126788409" typeof="mw:Extension/templatestyles" about="#mwt414" data-mw='{"name":"templatestyles","attrs":{"src":"Plainlist/styles.css"},"body":{"extsrc":""}}'>.mw-parser-output .plainlist ol,.mw-parser-output .plainlist ul{line-height:inherit;list-style:none;margin:0;padding:0}.mw-parser-output .plainlist ol li,.mw-parser-output .plainlist ul li{margin-bottom:0}</style></span><div class="side-box-flex"><div class="side-box-image"><figure class="mw-halign-center" typeof="mw:File"><span><img alt="" resource="//en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Gnome-mime-sound-openclipart.svg" src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/8/87/Gnome-mime-sound-openclipart.svg/60px-Gnome-mime-sound-openclipart.svg.png" decoding="async" data-file-width="160" data-file-height="160" data-file-type="drawing" height="50" width="50" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/8/87/Gnome-mime-sound-openclipart.svg/120px-Gnome-mime-sound-openclipart.svg.png 2x" class="mw-file-element"/></span><figcaption></figcaption></figure></div><div class="side-box-text plainlist"><div class="haudio"><div class="listen-file-header"><a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="//en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Grévys_zebra_(Sound_Effects).ogg" title="File:Grévys zebra (Sound Effects).ogg">Grévy's zebra call</a></div><div><span typeof="mw:File" class="mw-default-audio-height"><span><audio controls="" preload="none" data-mw-tmh="" height="32" width="232" style="width: 232px;" resource="//en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Grévys_zebra_(Sound_Effects).ogg" data-durationhint="12" class="mw-file-element"><source src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/8/8a/Gr%C3%A9vys_zebra_%28Sound_Effects%29.ogg" type='audio/ogg; codecs="vorbis"'/><source src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/transcoded/8/8a/Gr%C3%A9vys_zebra_%28Sound_Effects%29.ogg/Gr%C3%A9vys_zebra_%28Sound_Effects%29.ogg.mp3" type="audio/mpeg" data-transcodekey="mp3"/></audio></span></span></div><div class="description">The call of a Grevy's zebra at Lincoln Park Zoo, Chicago, Illinois.</div></div></div></div><div class="side-box-abovebelow"><hr/><i class="selfreference">Problems playing this file? See <a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="//en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Help:Media" title="Help:Media">media help</a>.</i></div></div><span class="mw-empty-elt" about="#mwt411"><link rel="mw:PageProp/Category" href="./Category:Articles_with_hAudio_microformats"/></span><p id="mwBAM">Zebras produce a number of vocalisations and noises. The plains zebra has a distinctive, <a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="//en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bark_(sound)" title="Bark (sound)" id="mwBAQ">barking</a> contact call heard as "a-<i id="mwBAU">ha</i>, a-<i id="mwBAY">ha</i>, a-<i id="mwBAc">ha</i>" or "kwa-ha, kaw-ha, ha, ha".<sup about="#mwt415" class="mw-ref reference" id="cite_ref-Grub_1981_28-4" rel="dc:references" typeof="mw:Extension/ref" data-mw='{"name":"ref","attrs":{"name":"Grub 1981"}}'><a href="#cite_note-Grub_1981-28" id="mwBAg"><span class="mw-reflink-text" id="mwBAk"><span class="cite-bracket" id="mwBAo">[</span>28<span class="cite-bracket" id="mwBAs">]</span></span></a></sup><sup about="#mwt416" class="mw-ref reference" id="cite_ref-Estes_1991_29-12" rel="dc:references" typeof="mw:Extension/ref" data-mw='{"name":"ref","attrs":{"name":"Estes 1991"}}'><a href="#cite_note-Estes_1991-29" id="mwBAw"><span class="mw-reflink-text" id="mwBA0"><span class="cite-bracket" id="mwBA4">[</span>29<span class="cite-bracket" id="mwBA8">]</span></span></a></sup> The mountain zebra may produce a similar sound while the call of Grévy's zebra has been described as "something like a <a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="//en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hippo" title="Hippo" class="mw-redirect" id="mwBBA">hippo</a>'s grunt combined with a donkey's wheeze". Loud snorting and rough "gasping" in zebras signals alarm. Squealing is usually made when in pain, but can also be heard in friendly interactions. Zebras also communicate with visual displays, and the flexibility of their lips allows them to make complex facial expressions. Visual displays also consist of head, ear, and tail postures. A zebra may signal an intention to kick by dropping back its ears and whipping its tail. Flattened ears, bared teeth and a waving head may be used as threatening gestures by stallions.<sup about="#mwt417" class="mw-ref reference" id="cite_ref-Estes_1991_29-13" rel="dc:references" typeof="mw:Extension/ref" data-mw='{"name":"ref","attrs":{"name":"Estes 1991"}}'><a href="#cite_note-Estes_1991-29" id="mwBBE"><span class="mw-reflink-text" id="mwBBI"><span class="cite-bracket" id="mwBBM">[</span>29<span class="cite-bracket" id="mwBBQ">]</span></span></a></sup></p><p id="mwBBU">Individuals may greet each other by mutually touching and rubbing, sniffing their genitals and resting their heads on their shoulders. They then may caress their shoulders against each other and lay their heads on one another. This greeting usually occurs between harem or territorial males or among bachelor males playing.<sup about="#mwt421" class="mw-ref reference" id="cite_ref-Estes_1991_29-14" rel="dc:references" typeof="mw:Extension/ref" data-mw='{"name":"ref","attrs":{"name":"Estes 1991"}}'><a href="#cite_note-Estes_1991-29" id="mwBBY"><span class="mw-reflink-text" id="mwBBc"><span class="cite-bracket" id="mwBBg">[</span>29<span class="cite-bracket" id="mwBBk">]</span></span></a></sup> Plains and mountain zebras strengthen their social bonds with <a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="//en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Social_grooming" title="Social grooming" id="mwBBo">grooming</a>. Members of a harem nibble and rake along the neck, shoulder, and back with their teeth and lips. Grooming usually occurs between mothers and foals and between stallions and mares. Grooming establishes social rank and eases aggressive behaviour,<sup about="#mwt422" class="mw-ref reference" id="cite_ref-Estes_1991_29-15" rel="dc:references" typeof="mw:Extension/ref" data-mw='{"name":"ref","attrs":{"name":"Estes 1991"}}'><a href="#cite_note-Estes_1991-29" id="mwBBs"><span class="mw-reflink-text" id="mwBBw"><span class="cite-bracket" id="mwBB0">[</span>29<span class="cite-bracket" id="mwBB4">]</span></span></a></sup><sup about="#mwt418" class="mw-ref reference" id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTECaro2016143_93-0" rel="dc:references" typeof="mw:Transclusion mw:Extension/ref" data-mw='{"name":"ref","attrs":{"group":"","name":"FOOTNOTECaro2016143"},"body":{"id":"mw-reference-text-cite_note-FOOTNOTECaro2016143-93"},"parts":[{"template":{"target":{"wt":"sfn","href":"./Template:Sfn"},"params":{"1":{"wt":"Caro"},"2":{"wt":"2016"},"p":{"wt":"143"}},"i":0}}]}'><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTECaro2016143-93" id="mwBB8"><span class="mw-reflink-text" id="mwBCA"><span class="cite-bracket" id="mwBCE">[</span>92<span class="cite-bracket" id="mwBCI">]</span></span></a></sup> although Grévy's zebras generally do not perform social grooming.<sup about="#mwt423" class="mw-ref reference" id="cite_ref-Churcher_1993_25-5" rel="dc:references" typeof="mw:Extension/ref" data-mw='{"name":"ref","attrs":{"name":"Churcher 1993"}}'><a href="#cite_note-Churcher_1993-25" id="mwBCM"><span class="mw-reflink-text" id="mwBCQ"><span class="cite-bracket" id="mwBCU">[</span>25<span class="cite-bracket" id="mwBCY">]</span></span></a></sup></p></section><section data-mw-section-id="12" id="mwBCc" aria-labelledby="Reproduction_and_parenting"><div class="mw-heading mw-heading3"><h3 id="Reproduction_and_parenting">Reproduction and parenting</h3></div><span class="mw-empty-elt" about="#mwt424" typeof="mw:Transclusion" id="mwBCg" data-mw='{"parts":[{"template":{"target":{"wt":"See also","href":"./Template:See_also"},"params":{"1":{"wt":"Horse breeding"}},"i":0}}]}'><link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1353705441" about="#mwt425" typeof="mw:Extension/templatestyles" data-mw='{"name":"templatestyles","attrs":{"src":"Module:Hatnote/styles.css"},"body":{"extsrc":""}}'/></span><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable" about="#mwt424" id="mwBCk">See also: <a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="//en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Horse_breeding" title="Horse breeding">Horse breeding</a></div><figure class="mw-default-size mw-halign-right" typeof="mw:File/Thumb" id="mwBCo"><a href="//en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Cape_Mountain_Zebras_(Equus_zebra)_mare_and_foal_suckling_..._(31281408687).jpg" class="mw-file-description" id="mwBCs"><img alt="Mountain zebra suckling a foal" resource="//en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Cape_Mountain_Zebras_(Equus_zebra)_mare_and_foal_suckling_..._(31281408687).jpg" src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/a/a4/Cape_Mountain_Zebras_%28Equus_zebra%29_mare_and_foal_suckling_..._%2831281408687%29.jpg/250px-Cape_Mountain_Zebras_%28Equus_zebra%29_mare_and_foal_suckling_..._%2831281408687%29.jpg" decoding="async" data-file-width="5021" data-file-height="3204" data-file-type="bitmap" height="160" width="250" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/a/a4/Cape_Mountain_Zebras_%28Equus_zebra%29_mare_and_foal_suckling_..._%2831281408687%29.jpg/500px-Cape_Mountain_Zebras_%28Equus_zebra%29_mare_and_foal_suckling_..._%2831281408687%29.jpg 2x" class="mw-file-element" id="mwBCw"/></a><figcaption id="mwBC0">Mountain zebra suckling a foal</figcaption></figure><p id="mwBC4">Among plains and mountain zebras, the adult females mate only with their harem stallion, while in Grévy's zebras, mating is more <a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="//en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Polygynandry" title="Polygynandry" id="mwBC8">promiscuous</a> and the males have larger testes for <a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="//en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sperm_competition" title="Sperm competition" id="mwBDA">sperm competition</a>.<sup about="#mwt428" class="mw-ref reference" id="cite_ref-Rubenstein_2010_91-1" rel="dc:references" typeof="mw:Extension/ref" data-mw='{"name":"ref","attrs":{"name":"Rubenstein 2010"},"body":{"id":"mw-reference-text-cite_note-Rubenstein_2010-91"}}'><a href="#cite_note-Rubenstein_2010-91" id="mwBDE"><span class="mw-reflink-text" id="mwBDI"><span class="cite-bracket" id="mwBDM">[</span>90<span class="cite-bracket" id="mwBDQ">]</span></span></a></sup><sup about="#mwt431" class="mw-ref reference" id="cite_ref-Ginsberg_1990_94-0" rel="dc:references" typeof="mw:Extension/ref" data-mw='{"name":"ref","attrs":{"name":"Ginsberg 1990"},"body":{"id":"mw-reference-text-cite_note-Ginsberg_1990-94"}}'><a href="#cite_note-Ginsberg_1990-94" id="mwBDU"><span class="mw-reflink-text" id="mwBDY"><span class="cite-bracket" id="mwBDc">[</span>93<span class="cite-bracket" id="mwBDg">]</span></span></a></sup> Female zebras have five to ten day long oestrous cycles; physical signs include a swollen, everted (inside out) labia and copious flows of urine and mucus. Upon reaching peak oestrous, mares spread-out their legs, lift their tails and open their mouths when in the presence of a male. Males assess the female's reproductive state with a curled lip and bared teeth (<a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="//en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Flehmen_response" title="Flehmen response" id="mwBDk">flehmen response</a>) and the female will solicit mating by backing in. Gestation is typically around a year. A few days to a month later, mares can return to oestrus.<sup about="#mwt432" class="mw-ref reference" id="cite_ref-Estes_1991_29-16" rel="dc:references" typeof="mw:Extension/ref" data-mw='{"name":"ref","attrs":{"name":"Estes 1991"}}'><a href="#cite_note-Estes_1991-29" id="mwBDo"><span class="mw-reflink-text" id="mwBDs"><span class="cite-bracket" id="mwBDw">[</span>29<span class="cite-bracket" id="mwBD0">]</span></span></a></sup> In harem-holding species, oestrus in a female becomes less noticeable to outside males as she gets older, hence competition for older females is virtually nonexistent.<sup about="#mwt433" class="mw-ref reference" id="cite_ref-Grub_1981_28-5" rel="dc:references" typeof="mw:Extension/ref" data-mw='{"name":"ref","attrs":{"name":"Grub 1981"}}'><a href="#cite_note-Grub_1981-28" id="mwBD4"><span class="mw-reflink-text" id="mwBD8"><span class="cite-bracket" id="mwBEA">[</span>28<span class="cite-bracket" id="mwBEE">]</span></span></a></sup></p><p id="mwBEI">Usually, a single foal is born, which is capable of running within an hour of birth.<sup about="#mwt434" class="mw-ref reference" id="cite_ref-MacDonald_9-10" rel="dc:references" typeof="mw:Extension/ref" data-mw='{"name":"ref","attrs":{"name":"MacDonald"}}'><a href="#cite_note-MacDonald-9" id="mwBEM"><span class="mw-reflink-text" id="mwBEQ"><span class="cite-bracket" id="mwBEU">[</span>9<span class="cite-bracket" id="mwBEY">]</span></span></a></sup> A newborn zebra will follow anything that moves, so new mothers prevent other mares from approaching their foals as they become more familiar with the mother's striping pattern, smell and voice.<sup about="#mwt435" class="mw-ref reference" id="cite_ref-Churcher_1993_25-6" rel="dc:references" typeof="mw:Extension/ref" data-mw='{"name":"ref","attrs":{"name":"Churcher 1993"}}'><a href="#cite_note-Churcher_1993-25" id="mwBEc"><span class="mw-reflink-text" id="mwBEg"><span class="cite-bracket" id="mwBEk">[</span>25<span class="cite-bracket" id="mwBEo">]</span></span></a></sup> At a few weeks old, foals begin to graze, but may continue to nurse for eight to thirteen months.<sup about="#mwt436" class="mw-ref reference" id="cite_ref-MacDonald_9-11" rel="dc:references" typeof="mw:Extension/ref" data-mw='{"name":"ref","attrs":{"name":"MacDonald"}}'><a href="#cite_note-MacDonald-9" id="mwBEs"><span class="mw-reflink-text" id="mwBEw"><span class="cite-bracket" id="mwBE0">[</span>9<span class="cite-bracket" id="mwBE4">]</span></span></a></sup> Living in an arid environment, Grévy's zebras have longer nursing intervals and young only begin to drink water three months after birth.<sup about="#mwt439" class="mw-ref reference" id="cite_ref-Becker_1990_95-0" rel="dc:references" typeof="mw:Extension/ref" data-mw='{"name":"ref","attrs":{"name":"Becker 1990"},"body":{"id":"mw-reference-text-cite_note-Becker_1990-95"}}'><a href="#cite_note-Becker_1990-95" id="mwBE8"><span class="mw-reflink-text" id="mwBFA"><span class="cite-bracket" id="mwBFE">[</span>94<span class="cite-bracket" id="mwBFI">]</span></span></a></sup></p><p id="mwBFM">In plains and mountain zebras, foals are cared for mostly by their mothers, but if threatened by pack-hunting hyenas and dogs, the entire group works together to protect all the young. The group forms a protective front with the foals in the centre, and the stallion will rush at predators that come too close.<sup about="#mwt440" class="mw-ref reference" id="cite_ref-Estes_1991_29-17" rel="dc:references" typeof="mw:Extension/ref" data-mw='{"name":"ref","attrs":{"name":"Estes 1991"}}'><a href="#cite_note-Estes_1991-29" id="mwBFQ"><span class="mw-reflink-text" id="mwBFU"><span class="cite-bracket" id="mwBFY">[</span>29<span class="cite-bracket" id="mwBFc">]</span></span></a></sup> In Grévy's zebras, young stay in "<a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="//en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Crèche_(zoology)" title="Crèche (zoology)" id="mwBFg">kindergartens</a>" when their mothers leave for water. These groups are tended to by the territorial male.<sup about="#mwt441" class="mw-ref reference" id="cite_ref-Becker_1990_95-1" rel="dc:references" typeof="mw:Extension/ref" data-mw='{"name":"ref","attrs":{"name":"Becker 1990"}}'><a href="#cite_note-Becker_1990-95" id="mwBFk"><span class="mw-reflink-text" id="mwBFo"><span class="cite-bracket" id="mwBFs">[</span>94<span class="cite-bracket" id="mwBFw">]</span></span></a></sup> A stallion may look after a foal in his territory to ensure that the mother stays, though it may not be his.<sup about="#mwt442" class="mw-ref reference" id="cite_ref-Rubenstein_1986_90-2" rel="dc:references" typeof="mw:Extension/ref" data-mw='{"name":"ref","attrs":{"name":"Rubenstein 1986"}}'><a href="#cite_note-Rubenstein_1986-90" id="mwBF0"><span class="mw-reflink-text" id="mwBF4"><span class="cite-bracket" id="mwBF8">[</span>89<span class="cite-bracket" id="mwBGA">]</span></span></a></sup> By contrast, plains zebra stallions are generally intolerant of foals that are not theirs and may practice <a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="//en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Infanticide_(zoology)" title="Infanticide (zoology)" id="mwBGE">infanticide</a> and <a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="//en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Feticide" title="Feticide" class="mw-redirect" id="mwBGI">feticide</a> via violence to the pregnant mare.<sup about="#mwt445" class="mw-ref reference" id="cite_ref-96" rel="dc:references" typeof="mw:Extension/ref" data-mw='{"name":"ref","attrs":{},"body":{"id":"mw-reference-text-cite_note-96"}}'><a href="#cite_note-96" id="mwBGM"><span class="mw-reflink-text" id="mwBGQ"><span class="cite-bracket" id="mwBGU">[</span>95<span class="cite-bracket" id="mwBGY">]</span></span></a></sup></p></section></section><section data-mw-section-id="13" id="mwBGc" aria-labelledby="Human_relations"><div class="mw-heading mw-heading2"><h2 id="Human_relations">Human relations</h2></div><section data-mw-section-id="14" id="mwBGg" aria-labelledby="Cultural_significance"><div class="mw-heading mw-heading3"><h3 id="Cultural_significance">Cultural significance</h3></div><figure class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Thumb" id="mwBGk"><a href="//en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Brandberg-Peintures_rupestres_San_(4).jpg" class="mw-file-description" id="mwBGo"><img alt="San rock art depicting a zebra" resource="//en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Brandberg-Peintures_rupestres_San_(4).jpg" src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/5/54/Brandberg-Peintures_rupestres_San_%284%29.jpg/250px-Brandberg-Peintures_rupestres_San_%284%29.jpg" decoding="async" data-file-width="5472" data-file-height="3648" data-file-type="bitmap" height="167" width="250" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/5/54/Brandberg-Peintures_rupestres_San_%284%29.jpg/500px-Brandberg-Peintures_rupestres_San_%284%29.jpg 2x" class="mw-file-element" id="mwBGs"/></a><figcaption id="mwBGw"><a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="//en.wikipedia.org/wiki/San_rock_art" title="San rock art" id="mwBG0">San rock art</a> depicting a zebra</figcaption></figure><p id="mwBG4">With their distinctive black-and-white stripes, zebras are among the most recognizable mammals. They have been associated with beauty and grace, with naturalist <a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="//en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Thomas_Pennant" title="Thomas Pennant" id="mwBG8">Thomas Pennant</a> describing them in 1781 as "the most elegant of quadrupeds". Zebras have been popular in photography, with some wildlife photographers describing them as the most photogenic animal. They have become staples in children's stories and wildlife-themed art, such as depictions of <a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="//en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Noah's_Ark" title="Noah's Ark" id="mwBHA">Noah's Ark</a>. In children's <a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="//en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alphabet_book" title="Alphabet book" id="mwBHE">alphabet books</a>, the animals are often used to represent the letter 'Z'. Zebra stripe patterns are popularly used for body paintings, dress, furniture and architecture.<sup about="#mwt446" class="mw-ref reference" id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEPlumbShaw201810–13,_40–41,_134–140,_189_97-0" rel="dc:references" typeof="mw:Transclusion mw:Extension/ref" data-mw='{"name":"ref","attrs":{"group":"","name":"FOOTNOTEPlumbShaw201810–13, 40–41, 134–140, 189"},"body":{"id":"mw-reference-text-cite_note-FOOTNOTEPlumbShaw201810–13,_40–41,_134–140,_189-97"},"parts":[{"template":{"target":{"wt":"sfn","href":"./Template:Sfn"},"params":{"1":{"wt":"Plumb"},"2":{"wt":"Shaw"},"3":{"wt":"2018"},"pp":{"wt":"10–13, 40–41, 134–140, 189"}},"i":0}}]}'><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEPlumbShaw201810–13,_40–41,_134–140,_189-97" id="mwBHI"><span class="mw-reflink-text" id="mwBHM"><span class="cite-bracket" id="mwBHQ">[</span>96<span class="cite-bracket" id="mwBHU">]</span></span></a></sup></p><p id="mwBHY">Zebras have been featured in <a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="//en.wikipedia.org/wiki/African_art" title="African art" id="mwBHc">African art</a> and <a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="//en.wikipedia.org/wiki/African_culture" title="African culture" class="mw-redirect" id="mwBHg">culture</a> for millennia. They are depicted in <a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="//en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rock_art" title="Rock art" id="mwBHk">rock art</a> in Southern Africa dating from 28,000 to 20,000 years ago, though less often than antelope species like <a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="//en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Common_eland" title="Common eland" id="mwBHo">eland</a>. How the zebra got its stripes has been the subject of <a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="//en.wikipedia.org/wiki/African_folklore" title="African folklore" class="mw-redirect" id="mwBHs">folk tales</a>, some of which involve it being scorched by fire. The <a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="//en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Maasai_people" title="Maasai people" id="mwBHw">Maasai</a> proverb "a man without culture is like a zebra without stripes" has become popular in Africa. The <a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="//en.wikipedia.org/wiki/San_people" title="San people" class="mw-redirect" id="mwBH0">San people</a> connected zebra stripes with water, rain and lightning, and <a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="//en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Water_spirit" title="Water spirit" id="mwBH4">water spirits</a> were conceived of having these markings.<sup about="#mwt449" class="mw-ref reference" id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEPlumbShaw201837–44_98-0" rel="dc:references" typeof="mw:Transclusion mw:Extension/ref" data-mw='{"name":"ref","attrs":{"group":"","name":"FOOTNOTEPlumbShaw201837–44"},"body":{"id":"mw-reference-text-cite_note-FOOTNOTEPlumbShaw201837–44-98"},"parts":[{"template":{"target":{"wt":"sfn","href":"./Template:Sfn"},"params":{"1":{"wt":"Plumb"},"2":{"wt":"Shaw"},"3":{"wt":"2018"},"pp":{"wt":"37–44"}},"i":0}}]}'><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEPlumbShaw201837–44-98" id="mwBH8"><span class="mw-reflink-text" id="mwBIA"><span class="cite-bracket" id="mwBIE">[</span>97<span class="cite-bracket" id="mwBII">]</span></span></a></sup></p><figure class="mw-default-size mw-halign-left" typeof="mw:File/Thumb" id="mwBIM"><a href="//en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Zebra_Stripes_Glen_Raven_1908.jpg" class="mw-file-description" id="mwBIQ"><img alt='Illustration of a business&apos;s "Zebra Stripes" logo' resource="//en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Zebra_Stripes_Glen_Raven_1908.jpg" src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/f/f2/Zebra_Stripes_Glen_Raven_1908.jpg/250px-Zebra_Stripes_Glen_Raven_1908.jpg" decoding="async" data-file-width="1920" data-file-height="2256" data-file-type="bitmap" height="223" width="190" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/f/f2/Zebra_Stripes_Glen_Raven_1908.jpg/500px-Zebra_Stripes_Glen_Raven_1908.jpg 2x" class="mw-file-element mw-file-upright" style="--mw-file-upright: 0.75" id="mwBIU"/></a><figcaption id="mwBIY"> "Zebra Stripes," trademark for the defunct <a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="//en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Glen_Raven,_Inc." title="Glen Raven, Inc." id="mwBIc">Glen Raven Cotton Mills Company</a></figcaption></figure><p id="mwBIg">For the <a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="//en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Shona_people" title="Shona people" id="mwBIk">Shona people</a>, the zebra is a <a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="//en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Totem" title="Totem" id="mwBIo">totem</a> animal and is glorified in a poem as an "iridescent and glittering creature". Its stripes have symbolised the union of male and female and at the ruined city of <a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="//en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Great_Zimbabwe" title="Great Zimbabwe" id="mwBIs">Great Zimbabwe</a>, zebra stripes decorate what is believed to be a <i id="mwBIw">domba</i>, a school meant to prepare girls for adulthood. In the <a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="//en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Shona_language" title="Shona language" id="mwBI0">Shona language</a>, the name <i id="mwBI4">madhuve</i> means "woman/women of the zebra totem" and is a name for girls in <a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="//en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Zimbabwe" title="Zimbabwe" id="mwBI8">Zimbabwe</a>. The plains zebra is the <a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="//en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_animal" title="National animal" class="mw-redirect" id="mwBJA">national animal</a> of Botswana and zebras have been depicted on stamps during <a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="//en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scramble_for_Africa" title="Scramble for Africa" id="mwBJE">colonial</a> and post-colonial Africa. For people of the <a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="//en.wikipedia.org/wiki/African_diaspora" title="African diaspora" id="mwBJI">African diaspora</a>, the zebra represented the politics of race and identity, being both black and white.<sup about="#mwt452" class="mw-ref reference" id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEPlumbShaw201845–50_99-0" rel="dc:references" typeof="mw:Transclusion mw:Extension/ref" data-mw='{"name":"ref","attrs":{"group":"","name":"FOOTNOTEPlumbShaw201845–50"},"body":{"id":"mw-reference-text-cite_note-FOOTNOTEPlumbShaw201845–50-99"},"parts":[{"template":{"target":{"wt":"sfn","href":"./Template:Sfn"},"params":{"1":{"wt":"Plumb"},"2":{"wt":"Shaw"},"3":{"wt":"2018"},"pp":{"wt":"45–50"}},"i":0}}]}'><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEPlumbShaw201845–50-99" id="mwBJM"><span class="mw-reflink-text" id="mwBJQ"><span class="cite-bracket" id="mwBJU">[</span>98<span class="cite-bracket" id="mwBJY">]</span></span></a></sup></p><p id="mwBJc">In cultures outside of its range, the zebra has been thought of as a more exotic alternative to the horse; the comic book character <a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="//en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sheena,_Queen_of_the_Jungle" title="Sheena, Queen of the Jungle" id="mwBJg">Sheena, Queen of the Jungle</a>, is depicted riding a zebra and explorer <a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="//en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Osa_Johnson" title="Osa Johnson" class="mw-redirect" id="mwBJk">Osa Johnson</a> was photographed riding one. The film <i id="mwBJo"><a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="//en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Racing_Stripes" title="Racing Stripes" id="mwBJs">Racing Stripes</a></i> features a captive zebra ostracised from the horses and ends up being ridden by a rebellious girl. Zebras have been featured as characters in animated films like <i id="mwBJw"><a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="//en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Khumba" title="Khumba" id="mwBJ0">Khumba</a></i>, <i id="mwBJ4"><a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="//en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Lion_King" title="The Lion King" id="mwBJ8">The Lion King</a></i> and the <a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="//en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Madagascar_(franchise)" title="Madagascar (franchise)" id="mwBKA"><i id="mwBKE">Madagascar</i> films</a> and television series such as <i id="mwBKI"><a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="//en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Zou_(TV_series)" title="Zou (TV series)" id="mwBKM">Zou</a></i>.<sup about="#mwt455" class="mw-ref reference" id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEPlumbShaw2018167–169,_188,_192–194,_200–201_100-0" rel="dc:references" typeof="mw:Transclusion mw:Extension/ref" data-mw='{"name":"ref","attrs":{"group":"","name":"FOOTNOTEPlumbShaw2018167–169, 188, 192–194, 200–201"},"body":{"id":"mw-reference-text-cite_note-FOOTNOTEPlumbShaw2018167–169,_188,_192–194,_200–201-100"},"parts":[{"template":{"target":{"wt":"sfn","href":"./Template:Sfn"},"params":{"1":{"wt":"Plumb"},"2":{"wt":"Shaw"},"3":{"wt":"2018"},"pp":{"wt":"167–169, 188, 192–194, 200–201"}},"i":0}}]}'><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEPlumbShaw2018167–169,_188,_192–194,_200–201-100" id="mwBKQ"><span class="mw-reflink-text" id="mwBKU"><span class="cite-bracket" id="mwBKY">[</span>99<span class="cite-bracket" id="mwBKc">]</span></span></a></sup></p><p id="mwBKg">Zebras have been popular subjects for <a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="//en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Abstract_art" title="Abstract art" id="mwBKk">abstract</a>, <a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="//en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Modernism" title="Modernism" id="mwBKo">modernist</a> and <a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="//en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Surrealism" title="Surrealism" id="mwBKs">surrealist</a> artists. Such art includes <a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="//en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Christopher_Wood_(painter)" title="Christopher Wood (painter)" id="mwBKw">Christopher Wood</a>'s <i id="mwBK0">Zebra and Parachute</i>, <a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="//en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lucian_Freud" title="Lucian Freud" id="mwBK4">Lucian Freud</a>'s <i id="mwBK8">The Painter's Room</i> and <i id="mwBLA">Quince on a Blue Table</i> and the various paintings of <a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="//en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mary_Fedden" title="Mary Fedden" id="mwBLE">Mary Fedden</a> and <a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="//en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sidney_Nolan" title="Sidney Nolan" id="mwBLI">Sidney Nolan</a>. <a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="//en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Victor_Vasarely" title="Victor Vasarely" id="mwBLM">Victor Vasarely</a> depicted zebras as black and white lines and connected in a <a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="//en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jigsaw_puzzle" title="Jigsaw puzzle" id="mwBLQ">jigsaw puzzle</a> fashion. <a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="//en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Carel_Weight" title="Carel Weight" id="mwBLU">Carel Weight</a>'s <i id="mwBLY">Escape of the Zebra from the Zoo during an Air Raid</i> was based on a real life incident of a zebra escaping during <a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="//en.wikipedia.org/wiki/London_Zoo#Second_World_War_1939–1945" title="London Zoo" id="mwBLc">the bombing of London Zoo</a> and consists of four comic book-like panels. Zebras have lent themselves to products and advertisements, including for 'Zebra Grate Polish' cleaning supplies by British manufacturer <a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="//en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Reckitt_and_Sons" title="Reckitt and Sons" id="mwBLg">Reckitt and Sons</a> and Japanese pen manufacturer <a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="//en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Zebra_(pen_manufacturer)" title="Zebra (pen manufacturer)" id="mwBLk">Zebra Co., Ltd.</a><sup about="#mwt458" class="mw-ref reference" id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEPlumbShaw2018128–131,_141–149_101-0" rel="dc:references" typeof="mw:Transclusion mw:Extension/ref" data-mw='{"name":"ref","attrs":{"group":"","name":"FOOTNOTEPlumbShaw2018128–131, 141–149"},"body":{"id":"mw-reference-text-cite_note-FOOTNOTEPlumbShaw2018128–131,_141–149-101"},"parts":[{"template":{"target":{"wt":"sfn","href":"./Template:Sfn"},"params":{"1":{"wt":"Plumb"},"2":{"wt":"Shaw"},"3":{"wt":"2018"},"pp":{"wt":"128–131, 141–149"}},"i":0}}]}'><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEPlumbShaw2018128–131,_141–149-101" id="mwBLo"><span class="mw-reflink-text" id="mwBLs"><span class="cite-bracket" id="mwBLw">[</span>100<span class="cite-bracket" id="mwBL0">]</span></span></a></sup></p></section><section data-mw-section-id="15" id="mwBL4" aria-labelledby="Captivity"><div class="mw-heading mw-heading3"><h3 id="Captivity">Captivity</h3></div><figure class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Thumb" id="mwBL8"><a href="//en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:George_Stubbs_-_Zebra_-_Google_Art_Project.jpg" class="mw-file-description" id="mwBMA"><img alt=" A portrait of a zebra by George Stubbs" resource="//en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:George_Stubbs_-_Zebra_-_Google_Art_Project.jpg" src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/f/f4/George_Stubbs_-_Zebra_-_Google_Art_Project.jpg/250px-George_Stubbs_-_Zebra_-_Google_Art_Project.jpg" decoding="async" data-file-width="5972" data-file-height="4753" data-file-type="bitmap" height="151" width="190" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/f/f4/George_Stubbs_-_Zebra_-_Google_Art_Project.jpg/500px-George_Stubbs_-_Zebra_-_Google_Art_Project.jpg 2x" class="mw-file-element mw-file-upright" style="--mw-file-upright: 0.75" id="mwBME"/></a><figcaption id="mwBMI"> <i id="mwBMM">Zebra</i> (1763) by <a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="//en.wikipedia.org/wiki/George_Stubbs" title="George Stubbs" id="mwBMQ">George Stubbs</a>. A portrait of <a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="//en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Queen_Charlotte" title="Queen Charlotte" class="mw-redirect" id="mwBMU">Queen Charlotte</a>'s zebra</figcaption></figure><p id="mwBMY">Zebras have been kept in captivity since at least the <a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="//en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Roman_Empire" title="Roman Empire" id="mwBMc">Roman Empire</a>. In later times, captive zebras have been shipped around the world, often for diplomatic reasons. In 1261, Sultan <a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="//en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Baibars" title="Baibars" class="mw-redirect" id="mwBMg">Baibars</a> of <a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="//en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bahri_dynasty" title="Bahri dynasty" class="mw-redirect" id="mwBMk">Egypt</a> established an embassy with <a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="//en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alfonso_X_of_Castile" title="Alfonso X of Castile" id="mwBMo">Alfonso X of Castile</a> and sent a zebra and other exotic animals as gifts. In 1417, a zebra was gifted to the Chinese people by Somalia and displayed before the <a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="//en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Yongle_Emperor" title="Yongle Emperor" id="mwBMs">Yongle Emperor</a>. The fourth <a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="//en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mughal_emperors" title="Mughal emperors" class="mw-redirect" id="mwBMw">Mughal</a> emperor <a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="//en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jahangir" title="Jahangir" id="mwBM0">Jahangir</a> received a zebra from Ethiopia in 1620 and <a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="//en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ustad_Mansur" title="Ustad Mansur" id="mwBM4">Ustad Mansur</a> made a painting of it. In the 1670s, <a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="//en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ethiopian_Empire" title="Ethiopian Empire" id="mwBM8">Ethiopian Emperor</a> <a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="//en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Yohannes_I" title="Yohannes I" id="mwBNA">Yohannes I</a> exported two zebras to the Dutch governor of <a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="//en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jakarta" title="Jakarta" id="mwBNE">Jakarta</a>. These animals would eventually be given by the Dutch to the <a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="//en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tokugawa_Shogunate" title="Tokugawa Shogunate" class="mw-redirect" id="mwBNI">Tokugawa Shogunate</a> of Japan.<sup about="#mwt461" class="mw-ref reference" id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEPlumbShaw2018[httpsbooksgooglecombooksid8GxaDwAAQBAJpgPT59_55–62],_65–66_102-0" rel="dc:references" typeof="mw:Transclusion mw:Extension/ref" data-mw='{"name":"ref","attrs":{"group":"","name":"FOOTNOTEPlumbShaw2018[httpsbooksgooglecombooksid8GxaDwAAQBAJpgPT59 55–62], 65–66"},"body":{"id":"mw-reference-text-cite_note-FOOTNOTEPlumbShaw2018[httpsbooksgooglecombooksid8GxaDwAAQBAJpgPT59_55–62],_65–66-102"},"parts":[{"template":{"target":{"wt":"sfn","href":"./Template:Sfn"},"params":{"1":{"wt":"Plumb"},"2":{"wt":"Shaw"},"3":{"wt":"2018"},"pp":{"wt":"[https://books.google.com/books?id=8GxaDwAAQBAJ&amp;pg=PT59 55–62], 65–66"}},"i":0}}]}'><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEPlumbShaw2018[httpsbooksgooglecombooksid8GxaDwAAQBAJpgPT59_55–62],_65–66-102" id="mwBNM"><span class="mw-reflink-text" id="mwBNQ"><span class="cite-bracket" id="mwBNU">[</span>101<span class="cite-bracket" id="mwBNY">]</span></span></a></sup></p><p id="mwBNc">When <a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="//en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Queen_Charlotte" title="Queen Charlotte" class="mw-redirect" id="mwBNg">Queen Charlotte</a> received a zebra as a wedding gift in 1762, the animal became a source of fascination for the people of Britain. Many flocked to see it at its <a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="//en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Paddock" title="Paddock" id="mwBNk">paddock</a> at <a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="//en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Buckingham_Palace" title="Buckingham Palace" id="mwBNo">Buckingham Palace</a>. It soon became the subject of humour and satire, being referred to as "The Queen's Ass", and was the subject of an oil painting by <a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="//en.wikipedia.org/wiki/George_Stubbs" title="George Stubbs" id="mwBNs">George Stubbs</a> in 1763. The zebra also gained a reputation for being ill-tempered and kicked at visitors.<sup about="#mwt464" class="mw-ref reference" id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEPlumbShaw201876–78,_81_103-0" rel="dc:references" typeof="mw:Transclusion mw:Extension/ref" data-mw='{"name":"ref","attrs":{"group":"","name":"FOOTNOTEPlumbShaw201876–78, 81"},"body":{"id":"mw-reference-text-cite_note-FOOTNOTEPlumbShaw201876–78,_81-103"},"parts":[{"template":{"target":{"wt":"sfn","href":"./Template:Sfn"},"params":{"1":{"wt":"Plumb"},"2":{"wt":"Shaw"},"3":{"wt":"2018"},"pp":{"wt":"76–78, 81"}},"i":0}}]}'><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEPlumbShaw201876–78,_81-103" id="mwBNw"><span class="mw-reflink-text" id="mwBN0"><span class="cite-bracket" id="mwBN4">[</span>102<span class="cite-bracket" id="mwBN8">]</span></span></a></sup> In 1882, Ethiopia sent a zebra to French president <a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="//en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jules_Grévy" title="Jules Grévy" id="mwBOA">Jules Grévy</a>, and the species it belonged to was named in his honour.<sup about="#mwt467" class="mw-ref reference" id="cite_ref-Prothero_2003_10-1" rel="dc:references" typeof="mw:Extension/ref" data-mw='{"name":"ref","attrs":{"name":"Prothero 2003"}}'><a href="#cite_note-Prothero_2003-10" id="mwBOE"><span class="mw-reflink-text" id="mwBOI"><span class="cite-bracket" id="mwBOM">[</span>10<span class="cite-bracket" id="mwBOQ">]</span></span></a></sup></p><figure class="mw-default-size mw-halign-left" typeof="mw:File/Thumb" id="mwBOU"><a href="//en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:WalterRothschildWithZebras.jpg" class="mw-file-description" id="mwBOY"><img alt="Walter Rothschild with a carriage drawn by four zebra" resource="//en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:WalterRothschildWithZebras.jpg" src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/2/2e/WalterRothschildWithZebras.jpg/250px-WalterRothschildWithZebras.jpg" decoding="async" data-file-width="800" data-file-height="426" data-file-type="bitmap" height="101" width="190" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/2/2e/WalterRothschildWithZebras.jpg/500px-WalterRothschildWithZebras.jpg 2x" class="mw-file-element mw-file-upright" style="--mw-file-upright: 0.75" id="mwBOc"/></a><figcaption id="mwBOg"><a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="//en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Walter_Rothschild" title="Walter Rothschild" class="mw-redirect" id="mwBOk">Walter Rothschild</a> with a zebra carriage</figcaption></figure><p id="mwBOo">Attempts to <a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="//en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Domesticate" title="Domesticate" class="mw-redirect" id="mwBOs">domesticate</a> zebras were largely unsuccessful. It is possible that having evolved under pressure from the many large predators of Africa, including early humans, they became more aggressive, thus making domestication more difficult.<sup about="#mwt473" class="mw-ref reference" id="cite_ref-104" rel="dc:references" typeof="mw:Extension/ref" data-mw='{"name":"ref","attrs":{},"body":{"id":"mw-reference-text-cite_note-104"}}'><a href="#cite_note-104" id="mwBOw"><span class="mw-reflink-text" id="mwBO0"><span class="cite-bracket" id="mwBO4">[</span>103<span class="cite-bracket" id="mwBO8">]</span></span></a></sup> However, zebras have been <a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="//en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Animal_training" title="Animal training" id="mwBPA">trained</a> throughout history. In Rome, zebras are recorded to have pulled chariots during <a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="//en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Amphitheatre" title="Amphitheatre" id="mwBPE">amphitheatre</a> games starting in the reign of <a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="//en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Caracalla" title="Caracalla" id="mwBPI">Caracalla</a> (198 to 217 AD).<sup about="#mwt468" class="mw-ref reference" id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEPlumbShaw201856_105-0" rel="dc:references" typeof="mw:Transclusion mw:Extension/ref" data-mw='{"name":"ref","attrs":{"group":"","name":"FOOTNOTEPlumbShaw201856"},"body":{"id":"mw-reference-text-cite_note-FOOTNOTEPlumbShaw201856-105"},"parts":[{"template":{"target":{"wt":"sfn","href":"./Template:Sfn"},"params":{"1":{"wt":"Plumb"},"2":{"wt":"Shaw"},"3":{"wt":"2018"},"p":{"wt":"56"}},"i":0}}]}'><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEPlumbShaw201856-105" id="mwBPM"><span class="mw-reflink-text" id="mwBPQ"><span class="cite-bracket" id="mwBPU">[</span>104<span class="cite-bracket" id="mwBPY">]</span></span></a></sup> In the late 19th century, the zoologist <a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="//en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Walter_Rothschild" title="Walter Rothschild" class="mw-redirect" id="mwBPc">Walter Rothschild</a> trained some zebras to draw a carriage in England, which he drove to Buckingham Palace to demonstrate that it can be done. However, he did not ride on them knowing that they were too small and aggressive.<sup about="#mwt476" class="mw-ref reference" id="cite_ref-106" rel="dc:references" typeof="mw:Extension/ref" data-mw='{"name":"ref","attrs":{},"body":{"id":"mw-reference-text-cite_note-106"}}'><a href="#cite_note-106" id="mwBPg"><span class="mw-reflink-text" id="mwBPk"><span class="cite-bracket" id="mwBPo">[</span>105<span class="cite-bracket" id="mwBPs">]</span></span></a></sup> In the early 20th century, <a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="//en.wikipedia.org/wiki/German_East_Africa" title="German East Africa" id="mwBPw">German colonial officers in East Africa</a> tried to use zebras for both driving and riding, with limited success.<sup about="#mwt479" class="mw-ref reference" id="cite_ref-107" rel="dc:references" typeof="mw:Extension/ref" data-mw='{"name":"ref","attrs":{},"body":{"id":"mw-reference-text-cite_note-107"}}'><a href="#cite_note-107" id="mwBP0"><span class="mw-reflink-text" id="mwBP4"><span class="cite-bracket" id="mwBP8">[</span>106<span class="cite-bracket" id="mwBQA">]</span></span></a></sup></p></section></section><section data-mw-section-id="16" id="mwBQE" aria-labelledby="Conservation"><div class="mw-heading mw-heading2"><h2 id="Conservation">Conservation</h2></div><figure class="mw-default-size mw-halign-left" typeof="mw:File/Thumb" id="mwBQI"><a href="//en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Equus_zebra_hartmannae_fur_skin.jpg" class="mw-file-description" id="mwBQM"><img alt="Mountain zebra hide" resource="//en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Equus_zebra_hartmannae_fur_skin.jpg" src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/b/be/Equus_zebra_hartmannae_fur_skin.jpg/250px-Equus_zebra_hartmannae_fur_skin.jpg" decoding="async" data-file-width="2442" data-file-height="3081" data-file-type="bitmap" height="240" width="190" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/b/be/Equus_zebra_hartmannae_fur_skin.jpg/500px-Equus_zebra_hartmannae_fur_skin.jpg 2x" class="mw-file-element mw-file-upright" style="--mw-file-upright: 0.75" id="mwBQQ"/></a><figcaption id="mwBQU"> Mountain zebra hide</figcaption></figure><p id="mwBQY">As of 2016–2019, the <a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="//en.wikipedia.org/wiki/IUCN_Red_List" title="IUCN Red List" id="mwBQc">IUCN Red List</a> of mammals lists Grévy's zebra as <a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="//en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Endangered_species" title="Endangered species" id="mwBQg">endangered</a>, the mountain zebra as <a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="//en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vulnerable_species" title="Vulnerable species" id="mwBQk">vulnerable</a> and the plains zebra as <a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="//en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Near-threatened_species" title="Near-threatened species" id="mwBQo">near-threatened</a>. Grévy's zebra populations are estimated at less than 2,000 mature individuals, but they are stable. Mountain zebras number near 35,000 individuals and their population appears to be increasing. Plains zebra are estimated to number 150,000–250,000 with a decreasing population trend. Human intervention has fragmented zebra ranges and populations. Zebras are threatened by hunting for their hide and meat, and <a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="//en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Habitat_destruction" title="Habitat destruction" id="mwBQs">habitat destruction</a>. They also compete with livestock and have their travelling routes obstruct by fences.<sup about="#mwt485" class="mw-ref reference" id="cite_ref-iucn-grevyi_108-0" rel="dc:references" typeof="mw:Extension/ref" data-mw='{"name":"ref","attrs":{"name":"iucn-grevyi"},"body":{"id":"mw-reference-text-cite_note-iucn-grevyi-108"}}'><a href="#cite_note-iucn-grevyi-108" id="mwBQw"><span class="mw-reflink-text" id="mwBQ0"><span class="cite-bracket" id="mwBQ4">[</span>107<span class="cite-bracket" id="mwBQ8">]</span></span></a></sup><sup about="#mwt488" class="mw-ref reference" id="cite_ref-iucn-zebra_109-0" rel="dc:references" typeof="mw:Extension/ref" data-mw='{"name":"ref","attrs":{"name":"iucn-zebra"},"body":{"id":"mw-reference-text-cite_note-iucn-zebra-109"}}'><a href="#cite_note-iucn-zebra-109" id="mwBRA"><span class="mw-reflink-text" id="mwBRE"><span class="cite-bracket" id="mwBRI">[</span>108<span class="cite-bracket" id="mwBRM">]</span></span></a></sup><sup about="#mwt491" class="mw-ref reference" id="cite_ref-iucn-quagga_110-0" rel="dc:references" typeof="mw:Extension/ref" data-mw='{"name":"ref","attrs":{"name":"iucn-quagga"},"body":{"id":"mw-reference-text-cite_note-iucn-quagga-110"}}'><a href="#cite_note-iucn-quagga-110" id="mwBRQ"><span class="mw-reflink-text" id="mwBRU"><span class="cite-bracket" id="mwBRY">[</span>109<span class="cite-bracket" id="mwBRc">]</span></span></a></sup> Civil wars in some countries have also caused declines in zebra populations.<sup about="#mwt494" class="mw-ref reference" id="cite_ref-Moehlman_2002_111-0" rel="dc:references" typeof="mw:Extension/ref" data-mw='{"name":"ref","attrs":{"name":"Moehlman 2002"},"body":{"id":"mw-reference-text-cite_note-Moehlman_2002-111"}}'><a href="#cite_note-Moehlman_2002-111" id="mwBRg"><span class="mw-reflink-text" id="mwBRk"><span class="cite-bracket" id="mwBRo">[</span>110<span class="cite-bracket" id="mwBRs">]</span></span></a></sup> By the early 20th century, zebra skins were being used to make rugs and chairs. In the 21st century, zebras may be taken by <a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="//en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Trophy_hunting" title="Trophy hunting" id="mwBRw">trophy hunters</a> as zebra skin rugs sell for $1,000 to $2,000. Trophy hunting was rare among African peoples though the San were known to hunt zebra for meat.<sup about="#mwt480" class="mw-ref reference" id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEPlumbShaw201841,_132–133_112-0" rel="dc:references" typeof="mw:Transclusion mw:Extension/ref" data-mw='{"name":"ref","attrs":{"group":"","name":"FOOTNOTEPlumbShaw201841, 132–133"},"body":{"id":"mw-reference-text-cite_note-FOOTNOTEPlumbShaw201841,_132–133-112"},"parts":[{"template":{"target":{"wt":"sfn","href":"./Template:Sfn"},"params":{"1":{"wt":"Plumb"},"2":{"wt":"Shaw"},"3":{"wt":"2018"},"pp":{"wt":"41, 132–133"}},"i":0}}]}'><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEPlumbShaw201841,_132–133-112" id="mwBR0"><span class="mw-reflink-text" id="mwBR4"><span class="cite-bracket" id="mwBR8">[</span>111<span class="cite-bracket" id="mwBSA">]</span></span></a></sup></p><figure class="mw-default-size mw-halign-right" typeof="mw:File/Thumb" id="mwBSE"><a href="//en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Samburu_Grevy's_zebra.jpg" class="mw-file-description" id="mwBSI"><img alt="A herd of Grévy's zebras in Samburu National Reserve" resource="//en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Samburu_Grevy's_zebra.jpg" src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/0/01/Samburu_Grevy%27s_zebra.jpg/250px-Samburu_Grevy%27s_zebra.jpg" decoding="async" data-file-width="1720" data-file-height="1044" data-file-type="bitmap" height="152" width="250" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/0/01/Samburu_Grevy%27s_zebra.jpg/500px-Samburu_Grevy%27s_zebra.jpg 2x" class="mw-file-element" id="mwBSM"/></a><figcaption id="mwBSQ"> Endangered Grévy's zebras in <a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="//en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Samburu_National_Reserve" title="Samburu National Reserve" id="mwBSU">Samburu National Reserve</a></figcaption></figure><p id="mwBSY">The <a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="//en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Quagga" title="Quagga" id="mwBSc">quagga</a> (<i id="mwBSg">E. quagga quagga</i>) population was hunted by early Dutch settlers and later by <a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="//en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Afrikaner" title="Afrikaner" class="mw-redirect" id="mwBSk">Afrikaners</a> to provide meat or for their skins. The skins were traded or used locally. The quagga was probably vulnerable to extinction due to its restricted range, and because they were easy to find in large groups. The last known wild quagga died in 1878.<sup about="#mwt497" class="mw-ref reference" id="cite_ref-Weddell_113-0" rel="dc:references" typeof="mw:Extension/ref" data-mw='{"name":"ref","attrs":{"name":"Weddell"},"body":{"id":"mw-reference-text-cite_note-Weddell-113"}}'><a href="#cite_note-Weddell-113" id="mwBSo"><span class="mw-reflink-text" id="mwBSs"><span class="cite-bracket" id="mwBSw">[</span>112<span class="cite-bracket" id="mwBS0">]</span></span></a></sup> The last captive quagga, a female in Amsterdam's <a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="//en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Natura_Artis_Magistra" title="Natura Artis Magistra" class="mw-redirect" id="mwBS4">Natura Artis Magistra</a> zoo, lived there from 9<span typeof="mw:Entity" id="mwBS8"> </span>May 1867 until it died on 12<span typeof="mw:Entity" id="mwBTA"> </span>August 1883.<sup about="#mwt500" class="mw-ref reference" id="cite_ref-ungulates_114-0" rel="dc:references" typeof="mw:Extension/ref" data-mw='{"name":"ref","attrs":{"name":"ungulates"},"body":{"id":"mw-reference-text-cite_note-ungulates-114"}}'><a href="#cite_note-ungulates-114" id="mwBTE"><span class="mw-reflink-text" id="mwBTI"><span class="cite-bracket" id="mwBTM">[</span>113<span class="cite-bracket" id="mwBTQ">]</span></span></a></sup> The <a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="//en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cape_mountain_zebra" title="Cape mountain zebra" id="mwBTU">Cape mountain zebra</a>, a subspecies of mountain zebra, nearly went extinct due to hunting and habitat destruction, with less than 50 individuals left by the 1950s. Protections from <a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="//en.wikipedia.org/wiki/South_African_National_Parks" title="South African National Parks" id="mwBTY">South African National Parks</a> allowed the population to rise to 2,600 by the 2010s.<sup about="#mwt503" class="mw-ref reference" id="cite_ref-115" rel="dc:references" typeof="mw:Extension/ref" data-mw='{"name":"ref","attrs":{},"body":{"id":"mw-reference-text-cite_note-115"}}'><a href="#cite_note-115" id="mwBTc"><span class="mw-reflink-text" id="mwBTg"><span class="cite-bracket" id="mwBTk">[</span>114<span class="cite-bracket" id="mwBTo">]</span></span></a></sup></p><p id="mwBTs">Zebras can be found in numerous protected areas. Important areas for Grévy's zebra include <a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="//en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Yabelo_Wildlife_Sanctuary" title="Yabelo Wildlife Sanctuary" id="mwBTw">Yabelo Wildlife Sanctuary</a> and <a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="//en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chelbi_Wildlife_Sanctuary" title="Chelbi Wildlife Sanctuary" class="mw-redirect" id="mwBT0">Chelbi Sanctuary</a> in Ethiopia and <a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="//en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Buffalo_Springs_National_Reserve" title="Buffalo Springs National Reserve" id="mwBT4">Buffalo Springs</a>, <a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="//en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Samburu_National_Reserve" title="Samburu National Reserve" id="mwBT8">Samburu</a> and <a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="//en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Shaba_National_Reserve" title="Shaba National Reserve" id="mwBUA">Shaba</a> National Reserves in <a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="//en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kenya" title="Kenya" id="mwBUE">Kenya</a>.<sup about="#mwt504" class="mw-ref reference" id="cite_ref-iucn-grevyi_108-1" rel="dc:references" typeof="mw:Extension/ref" data-mw='{"name":"ref","attrs":{"name":"iucn-grevyi"}}'><a href="#cite_note-iucn-grevyi-108" id="mwBUI"><span class="mw-reflink-text" id="mwBUM"><span class="cite-bracket" id="mwBUQ">[</span>107<span class="cite-bracket" id="mwBUU">]</span></span></a></sup> The plains zebra inhabits the <a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="//en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Serengeti_National_Park" title="Serengeti National Park" id="mwBUY">Serengeti National Park</a> in Tanzania, <a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="//en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tsavo" title="Tsavo" id="mwBUc">Tsavo</a> and <a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="//en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Masai_Mara" title="Masai Mara" class="mw-redirect" id="mwBUg">Masai Mara</a> in Kenya, <a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="//en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hwange_National_Park" title="Hwange National Park" id="mwBUk">Hwange National Park</a> in Zimbabwe, <a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="//en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Etosha_National_Park" title="Etosha National Park" id="mwBUo">Etosha National Park</a> in <a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="//en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Namibia" title="Namibia" id="mwBUs">Namibia</a>, and <a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="//en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kruger_National_Park" title="Kruger National Park" id="mwBUw">Kruger National Park</a> in <a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="//en.wikipedia.org/wiki/South_Africa" title="South Africa" id="mwBU0">South Africa</a>.<sup about="#mwt505" class="mw-ref reference" id="cite_ref-iucn-quagga_110-1" rel="dc:references" typeof="mw:Extension/ref" data-mw='{"name":"ref","attrs":{"name":"iucn-quagga"}}'><a href="#cite_note-iucn-quagga-110" id="mwBU4"><span class="mw-reflink-text" id="mwBU8"><span class="cite-bracket" id="mwBVA">[</span>109<span class="cite-bracket" id="mwBVE">]</span></span></a></sup> Mountain zebras are protected in <a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="//en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mountain_Zebra_National_Park" title="Mountain Zebra National Park" id="mwBVI">Mountain Zebra National Park</a>, <a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="//en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Karoo_National_Park" title="Karoo National Park" id="mwBVM">Karoo National Park</a> and <a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="//en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Goegap_Nature_Reserve" title="Goegap Nature Reserve" id="mwBVQ">Goegap Nature Reserve</a> in South Africa as well as Etosha and <a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="//en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Namib-Naukluft_Park" title="Namib-Naukluft Park" class="mw-redirect" id="mwBVU">Namib-Naukluft Park</a> in Namibia.<sup about="#mwt506" class="mw-ref reference" id="cite_ref-iucn-zebra_109-1" rel="dc:references" typeof="mw:Extension/ref" data-mw='{"name":"ref","attrs":{"name":"iucn-zebra"}}'><a href="#cite_note-iucn-zebra-109" id="mwBVY"><span
